Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (0≤x≤n, 0≤y≤m)
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle containing point (x,y) with exact aspect ratio a/b
3. The sub-rectangle must have dimensions (w,h) where w/h = a/b, so w = (a/b) * h
4. Since dimensions must be integers, we need w/h = a/b, which means w*b = h*a
5. We want the rectangle closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance, and if tied, lexicographically smallest

## Mathematical Analysis

Given ratio a/b, we need w/h = a/b, so w = (a/g) * k and h = (b/g) * k for some integer k and gcd(a,b) = g.

So w = a*k/gcd(a,b) and h = b*k/gcd(a,b) for some positive integer k.

## Constraints

For a rectangle with width w and height h:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ → x₂ - x₁ ≥ 0 and x₂ ≥ x ≥ x₁
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ → y₂ - y₁ ≥ 0 and y₂ ≥ y ≥ y₁
- x₁ ≥ 0, x₂ ≤ n, y₁ ≥ 0, y₂ ≤ m

So we need:
- w = x₂ - x₁ ≤ n - x₁, w ≤ n - x₁
- h = y₂ - y₁ ≤ m - y₁, h ≤ m - y₁

## Solution Strategy

1. Find all possible dimensions (w,h) where w/h = a/b
2. For each valid (w,h), find if we can place the rectangle containing (x,y)
3. Among all valid solutions, pick the one closest to (x,y)
4. If tied, pick lexicographically smallest

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Calculate gcd(a,b) to get the fundamental ratio
2. For each possible k, get dimensions w = a*k/gcd(a,b), h = b*k/gcd(a,b)
3. For each valid (w,h), check if we can place it containing (x,y)
4. Calculate the center of each valid rectangle and distance to (x,y)
5. Return the best one

```python
import math
from collections import deque

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def solve(n, m, x, y, a, b):
    g = gcd(a, b)
    a_red = a // g
    b_red = b // g
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = -1, -1, -1, -1
    best_dist_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Try different values of k
    # w = a_red * k, h = b_red * k
    # We need to find valid k such that we can place the rectangle
    
    # The rectangle must contain (x,y), so we can place it with:
    # x1 = x - w/2, x2 = x + w/2 (approximately)
    # y1 = y - h/2, y2 = y + h/2 (approximately)
    
    # But we need integer coordinates and constraints
    # The width w must be ≤ n, height h must be ≤ m
    
    # We can place the rectangle such that:
    # x1 ≥ 0, x2 ≤ n, so w ≤ n, x1 ≤ x ≤ x2
    # y1 ≥ 0, y2 ≤ m, so h ≤ m, y1 ≤ y ≤ y2
    
    # For a given w,h, the x1 can be from max(0, x-w) to min(x, n-w)
    # For a given w,h, the y1 can be from max(0, y-h) to min(y, m-h)
    
    # Actually, we need x1 ≤ x ≤ x2 and x2 = x1 + w
    # So x1 ≤ x ≤ x1 + w → x - w ≤ x1 ≤ x
    # Similarly: y - h ≤ y1 ≤ y
    
    # Therefore x1 ∈ [max(0, x-w), min(x, n-w)] and y1 ∈ [max(0, y-h), min(y, m-h)]
    
    # We need max(0, x-w) ≤ min(x, n-w) and max(0, y-h) ≤ min(y, m-h)
    
    # Let's try reasonable values of k
    # Since w = a_red * k, we need a_red * k ≤ n
    # So k ≤ n / a_red
    # Similarly k ≤ m / b_red
    
    # But k can be very large, so we need to find a bound
    
    # Actually, we only need to check values of k that give us non-empty placement
    
    # Upper bound for k: k <= min(n // a_red, m // b_red) if we want non-zero dimensions
    # But we also need to ensure we can place it containing (x,y)
    
    # Let's find all valid k values
    max_k = min(n // a_red, m // b_red) + 1
    
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        w = a_red * k
        h = b_red * k
        
        # Check if we can place this rectangle containing (x,y)
        # x1 must satisfy: x - w ≤ x1 ≤ x ≤ x1 + w and x1 ≥ 0, x1 + w ≤ n
        # So x1 ≥ max(0, x - w) and x1 ≤ min(x, n - w)
        
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        
        # Similarly for y
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        # If both ranges are valid, we can place this rectangle
        if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
            # For each valid x1, y1, the rectangle is (x1, y1, x1+w, y1+h)
            # We want to minimize distance to (x,y)
            # Distance squared: (x - (x1 + w/2))² + (y - (y1 + h/2))²
            
            # To minimize distance, we should place center as close to (x,y) as possible
            # Center is at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
            
            # For fixed w,h, we want to minimize (x - (x1 + w/2))² + (y - (y1 + h/2))²
            # This happens when x1 + w/2 is as close to x as possible, and y1 + h/2 is as close to y as possible
            
            # So x1 should be as close to x - w/2 as possible within bounds
            # y1 should be as close to y - h/2 as possible within bounds
            
            x1_opt = x1_min + max(0, min(x1_max, x - w/2 - 0.000001) - x1_min)
            # Better to just check boundary values
            
            # Since we want lexicographically smallest when tie, we should check all possibilities
            # But we can optimize: for fixed w,h, the optimal placement is determined
            
            # Find best x1 in [x1_min, x1_max]
            x1_opt = x1_min
            # We want x1 as close to (x - w/2) as possible
            target_x1 = x - w / 2
            if target_x1 < x1_min:
                x1_opt = x1_min
            elif target_x1 > x1_max:
                x1_opt = x1_max
            else:
                x1_opt = int(round(target_x1))
                if x1_opt < x1_min:
                    x1_opt = x1_min
                elif x1_opt > x1_max:
                    x1_opt = x1_max
            
            # Find best y1 in [y1_min, y1_max]
            y1_opt = y1_min
            target_y1 = y - h / 2
            if target_y1 < y1_min:
                y1_opt = y1_min
            elif target_y1 > y1_max:
                y1_opt = y1_max
            else:
                y1_opt = int(round(target_y1))
                if y1_opt < y1_min:
                    y1_opt = y1_min
                elif y1_opt > y1_max:
                    y1_opt = y1_max
            
            # But we need to ensure the point (x,y) is inside
            # x1 ≤ x ≤ x1 + w and y1 ≤ y ≤ y1 + h
            # We have already ensured this by constraints
            
            x2 = x1_opt + w
            y2 = y1_opt + h
            
            # Check if valid
            if x2 <= n and y2 <= m and x1_opt >= 0 and y1_opt >= 0:
                # Calculate distance squared
                center_x = x1_opt + w / 2
                center_y = y1_opt + h / 2
                dist_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
                
                # Check if this is better
                if (best_dist_squared == float('inf') or 
                    dist_squared < best_dist_squared or 
                    (dist_squared == best_dist_squared and 
                     (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2) > (x1_opt, y1_opt, x2, y2))):
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_opt, y1_opt, x2, y2
                    best_dist_squared = dist_squared
    
    # If no solution found, try k=0 (zero rectangle), but problem says a,b ≥ 1
    # So we should always have at least one solution for k≥1
    
    return best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2

# Read input
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
x1, y1, x2, y2 = solve(n, m, x, y, a, b)
print(x1, y1, x2, y2)

Wait, I think there's an issue with my approach. Let me reconsider the problem.

The key insight is that for a given aspect ratio a:b, the dimensions must satisfy width/height = a/b, so width = k*a/gcd(a,b) and height = k*b/gcd(a,b) for some positive integer k.

But I need to be more careful about the constraints.

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def solve(n, m, x, y, a, b):
    g = gcd(a, b)
    a_red = a // g
    b_red = b // g
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = -1, -1, -1, -1
    best_dist_squared = float('inf')
    
    # Try different values of k
    # w = a_red * k, h = b_red * k
    # We need w >= 1, h >= 1
    
    # Upper bound for k
    max_k = min(n // a_red, m // b_red) if a_red > 0 and b_red > 0 else 0
    
    # We should also consider that the rectangle must contain (x,y)
    # For a given w,h, we need:
    # x1 >= 0, x1 + w <= n, so x1 <= n - w
    # x1 <= x <= x1 + w, so x - w <= x1 <= x
    # Similarly for y
    
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        w = a_red * k
        h = b_red * k
        
        # Check if we can place this rectangle with (x,y) inside
        # x1 must be >= max(0, x - w) and <= min(x, n - w)
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        
        # y1 must be >= max(0, y - h) and <= min(y, m - h)
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For this w,h, find the placement that minimizes distance to (x,y)
        # Center of rectangle is at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
        # We want to minimize (x - (x1 + w/2))² + (y - (y1 + h/2))²
        
        # For x1: we want x1 + w/2 as close to x as possible
        # So x1 should be as close to x - w/2 as possible
        
        x1_opt = x1_min
        if x1_min <= x - w/2 <= x1_max:
            x1_opt = int(round(x - w/2))
            if x1_opt < x1_min:
                x1_opt = x1_min
            elif x1_opt > x1_max:
                x1_opt = x1_max
        else:
            if x - w/2 < x1_min:
                x1_opt = x1_min
            else:
                x1_opt = x1_max
        
        y1_opt = y1_min
        if y1_min <= y - h/2 <= y1_max:
            y1_opt = int(round(y - h/2))
            if y1_opt < y1_min:
                y1_opt = y1_min
            elif y1_opt > y1_max:
                y1_opt = y1_max
        else:
            if y - h/2 < y1_min:
                y1_opt = y1_min
            else:
                y1_opt = y1_max
        
        # Ensure (x,y) is inside
        if x1_opt + w > n or y1_opt + h > m:
            continue
            
        x2 = x1_opt + w
        y2 = y1_opt + h
        
        # Calculate distance to center
        center_x = x1_opt + w / 2
        center_y = y1_opt + h / 2
        dist_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
        
        # Check if better than current best
        if best_dist_squared == float('inf') or dist_squared < best_dist_squared:
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_opt, y1_opt, x2, y2
            best_dist_squared = dist_squared
        elif dist_squared == best_dist_squared:
            # Lexicographically smallest: compare (x1, y1, x2, y2)
            current = (x1_opt, y1_opt, x2, y2)
            best = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
            if current < best:
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_opt, y1_opt, x2, y2
    
    return best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2

# Read input
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
x1, y1, x2, y2 = solve(n, m, x, y, a, b)
print(x1, y1, x2, y2)

        # So x1 should be as close to x - w/2 as possible

            if x1_opt < x1_min:
                x1_opt = x1_min
            elif x1_opt > x1_max:
                x1_opt = x1_max
        else:
            if x - w/2 < x1_min:
                x1_opt = x1_min
            else:
                x1_opt = x1_max
        
        y1_opt = y1_min
        if y1_min <= y - h/2 <= y1_max:
            y1_opt = int(round(y - h/2))
            if y1_opt < y1_min:
                y1_opt = y1_min
            elif y1_opt > y1_max:
                y1_opt = y1_max
        else:
            if y - h/2 < y1_min:
                y1_opt = y1_min
            else:
                y1_opt = y1_max

        if x1_opt + w > n or y1_opt + h > m:
            continue
            
        x2 = x1_opt + w
        y2 = y1_opt + h

        center_x = x1_opt + w / 2
        center_y = y1_opt + h / 2
        dist_squared = (x - center_x) ** 2 + (y - center_y) ** 2
        
        # Check if better than current best

            best = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
            if current < best:
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_opt, y1_opt, x2, y2
    
    return best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0≤x≤n, 0≤y≤m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the given point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
   - Is as close as possible to (x,y) in Euclidean distance to center
   - If still tied, choose lexicographically smallest (x₁,y₁,x₂,y₂)

## Mathematical Analysis

The ratio condition is: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b

This means: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)

Let's define:
- width = w = x₂ - x₁
- height = h = y₂ - y₁

So: b×w = a×h, which means w = (a/b)×h

Since w and h must be integers, h must be divisible by b, and w must be divisible by a.
Let h = k×b, then w = k×a for some integer k ≥ 1.

So the rectangle dimensions are w×h = k×a × k×b = k×(a×b)

## Constraints

The rectangle must contain (x,y), so:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ → x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + w = x₁ + k×a
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ → y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + h = y₁ + k×b

This gives us:
1. x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + k×a → x - k×a ≤ x₁ ≤ x
2. y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + k×b → y - k×b ≤ y₁ ≤ y

We also have bounds:
- 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ n - w = n - k×a
- 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ m - h = m - k×b

## Finding Valid k

For a given k, we need:
- x₁ ∈ [max(0, x - k×a), min(x, n - k×a)]
- y₁ ∈ [max(0, y - k×b), min(y, m - k×b)]

For there to be a valid rectangle with this k, we need:
- max(0, x - k×a) ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
- max(0, y - k×b) ≤ min(y, m - k×b)

This simplifies to:
- x - k×a ≤ n - k×a (always true if x ≤ n)
- x - k×a ≤ x (always true)
- x ≥ 0 (always true)
- y - k×b ≤ m - k×b (always true if y ≤ m)
- y - k×b ≤ y (always true)

So we need:
- x - k×a ≥ 0 → k ≤ x/a
- x - k×a ≤ n - k×a → x ≤ n (given)
- Similarly for y: k ≤ y/b

Actually, let me reconsider the bounds more carefully:

For x₁ to exist:
- We need max(0, x - k×a) ≤ min(x, n - k×a)

This requires:
- max(0, x - k×a) ≤ n - k×a
- max(0, x - k×a) ≤ x (always true)

And:
- max(0, x - k×a) ≤ min(x, n - k×a)

Breaking it down:
1. If x - k×a ≤ 0, then x₁ ≥ 0, so we need 0 ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
2. If x - k×a > 0, then x₁ ≥ x - k×a, and we need x - k×a ≤ min(x, n - k×a)

For the second case: x - k×a ≤ x (true) and x - k×a ≤ n - k×a → x ≤ n (true)

For the first case: we need 0 ≤ n - k×a → k×a ≤ n → k ≤ n/a

Similarly for y: k ≤ m/b

So k must satisfy: k ≤ min(x/a, y/b, n/a, m/b)

But we also need the rectangle dimensions to fit: k×a ≤ n, k×b ≤ m

So valid k values are: k = 1, 2, ..., min(⌊x/a⌋, ⌊y/b⌋, ⌊n/a⌋, ⌊m/b⌋)

## Algorithm

1. Find all valid k values: k from 1 to min(⌊x/a⌋, ⌊y/b⌋, ⌊n/a⌋, ⌊m/b⌋)
2. For each k, find the valid range of x₁ and y₁
3. For each valid (x₁, y₁), calculate the center and Euclidean distance
4. Choose the one with minimum distance, then lexicographically smallest

Actually, we can optimize - for each k, we want to find the rectangle that is closest to (x,y). The center is ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁ + x₁ + k×a)/2, (y₁ + y₁ + k×b)/2) = (x₁ + k×a/2, y₁ + k×b/2)

Distance squared: (x₁ + k×a/2 - x)² + (y₁ + k×b/2 - y)²

To minimize this, we want x₁ as close as possible to x - k×a/2, and y₁ as close as possible to y - k×b/2.

## Implementation Strategy

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find valid k values
max_k = min(x // a, y // b, n // a, m // b)
if max_k == 0:
    # No valid rectangle possible
    print(0, 0, 0, 0)
else:
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Try all valid k values
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        w = k * a
        h = k * b
        
        # Valid x1 range: x1 <= x <= x1 + w
        # So x1 >= x - w and x1 <= x
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        
        # Valid y1 range: y1 <= y <= y1 + h
        # So y1 >= y - h and y1 <= y
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        # If no valid ranges, skip
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # Find x1 that minimizes distance to center
        # Center x = x1 + w/2, so distance in x-direction = |x1 + w/2 - x|
        # We want x1 as close as possible to x - w/2
        x1_target = x - w / 2
        x1_opt = int(max(x1_min, min(x1_max, x1_target)))
        
        # For lexicographically smallest, if multiple with same distance, prefer smaller x1
        # But we want to minimize distance first
        best_dist_sq_k = float('inf')
        
        # Try the best x1 and y1 values
        for x1_candidate in [x1_min, x1_max]:
            if x1_min <= x1_candidate <= x1_max:
                for y1_candidate in [y1_min, y1_max]:
                    if y1_min <= y1_candidate <= y1_max:
                        x2 = x1_candidate + w
                        y2 = y1_candidate + h
                        center_x = (x1_candidate + x2) / 2
                        center_y = (y1_candidate + y2) / 2
                        dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                        
                        if dist_sq < best_dist_sq_k:
                            best_dist_sq_k = dist_sq
                            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_candidate, y1_candidate, x2, y2
                        elif dist_sq == best_dist_sq_k:
                            # Lexicographic comparison: (x1, y1, x2, y2)
                            current_tuple = (x1_candidate, y1_candidate, x2, y2)
                            prev_tuple = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
                            if current_tuple < prev_tuple:
                                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_candidate, y1_candidate, x2, y2
        
        # Compare with best overall
        if best_dist_sq_k < best_dist_sq:
            best_dist_sq = best_dist_sq_k
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_candidate, y1_candidate, x2, y2
        elif best_dist_sq_k == best_dist_sq:
            # Lexicographic comparison
            current_tuple = (x1_candidate, y1_candidate, x2, y2)
            prev_tuple = (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
            if current_tuple < prev_tuple:
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_candidate, y1_candidate, x2, y2
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

Wait, I'm making this too complicated. Let me simplify:

For each k, we just need to find the valid x1 and y1 that minimize the distance to center.

The center is at (x1 + k*a/2, y1 + k*b/2).

For x1, we want to make x1 + k*a/2 as close as possible to x, so x1 ≈ x - k*a/2.

Similarly, y1 ≈ y - k*b/2.

For the valid range, we pick the value closest to the target within bounds.

Actually, there's a simpler approach - for each k, we just try the values that minimize distance, and it's safe to just use nearest integer.

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

max_k = min(x // a, y // b, n // a, m // b)
if max_k == 0:
    print(0, 0, 0, 0)
else:
    best_result = None
    
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        w = k * a
        h = k * b
        
        # Valid ranges for x1, y1
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # Find best x1 and y1 to minimize distance to center
        # Center at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
        # Distance squared = (x1 + w/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + h/2 - y)^2
        
        # Optimal x1 is x - w/2, constrained by [x1_min, x1_max]
        x1_opt = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, round(x - w / 2)))
        # In case of exact round, work with the actual values
        x1_candidate = x1_opt
        
        # For y1
        y1_opt = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, round(y - h / 2)))
        y1_candidate = y1_opt
        
        # Make sure they're actually in range
        if x1_min <= x1_candidate <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_candidate <= y1_max:
            x2 = x1_candidate + w
            y2 = y1_candidate + h
            
            # Calculate distance
            center_x = (x1_candidate + x2) / 2
            center_y = (y1_candidate + y2) / 2
            dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
            
            if best_result is None or dist_sq < best_result[0] or (dist_sq == best_result[0] and (x1_candidate, y1_candidate, x2, y2) < best_result[1]):
                best_result = (dist_sq, (x1_candidate, y1_candidate, x2, y2))
    
    print(best_result[1][0], best_result[1][1], best_result[1][2], best_result[1][3])
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the given point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
   - Is as close as possible to the center of the point (x,y)
   - If ties, choose lexicographically smallest

## Mathematical Analysis

The ratio condition: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b
This means: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)

Let's define:
- width = w = x₂ - x₁ (must be positive)
- height = h = y₂ - y₁ (must be positive)

So: b×w = a×h, which means w/h = a/b

This implies w = k×a and h = k×b for some positive integer k.

## Constraints

For the sub-rectangle to contain (x,y):
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ → x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + w
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ → y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + h

This gives us:
- x₁ ≤ x and x₁ ≥ x - w (since x ≤ x₁ + w)
- y₁ ≤ y and y₁ ≥ y - h (since y ≤ y₁ + h)

So: max(0, x - w) ≤ x₁ ≤ min(x, n - w)
And: max(0, y - h) ≤ y₁ ≤ min(y, m - h)

## Algorithm Steps

1. For each possible scaling factor k, we get w = k×a, h = k×b
2. Check if such a rectangle can contain (x,y)
3. For valid k values, find the one that minimizes distance to center
4. If tie, choose lexicographically smallest

## Distance to Center

The center of rectangle is ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁+x₁+w)/2, (y₁+y₁+h)/2)
Distance squared = ((x₁ + w/2 - x)² + (y₁ + h/2 - y)²)

## Optimization

Instead of checking all possible k, we need to find valid ranges for k.

For a given k:
- w = k×a, h = k×b
- x₁ must satisfy: max(0, x - k×a) ≤ x₁ ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
- y₁ must satisfy: max(0, y - k×b) ≤ y₁ ≤ min(y, m - k×b)

Valid k must satisfy:
- k×a ≤ n (so w ≤ n)
- k×b ≤ m (so h ≤ m)
- x - k×a ≤ n (always true for valid k)
- y - k×b ≤ m (always true for valid k)

So k ≤ min(n/a, m/b)

Also, we need valid range for x₁ and y₁:
- max(0, x - k×a) ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
- max(0, y - k×b) ≤ min(y, m - k×b)

This means:
- If x - k×a > 0, then x - k×a ≤ n - k×a → x ≤ n (always true)
- We need max(0, x - k×a) ≤ min(x, n - k×a)

Since x ≤ x + k×a, we need to ensure the range is non-empty.

Actually, simpler: we need the valid intervals to be non-empty.

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # For each k, we get w = k*a, h = k*b
    # We need k*a <= n and k*b <= m
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_distance_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Try different values of k
    # We need to find valid k such that the rectangle can contain (x,y)
    
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        w = k * a
        h = k * b
        
        # Check if rectangle can contain (x,y)
        # x1 can be from max(0, x - w) to min(x, n - w)
        x1_min = max(0, x - w)
        x1_max = min(x, n - w)
        
        # y1 can be from max(0, y - h) to min(y, m - h)
        y1_min = max(0, y - h)
        y1_max = min(y, m - h)
        
        # Check if valid ranges exist
        if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
            continue
            
        # For this k, we can choose any valid (x1, y1)
        # We want to minimize distance to center
        
        # Center of rectangle is at (x1 + w/2, y1 + h/2)
        # Distance squared from (x,y) is ((x1 + w/2 - x)^2 + (y1 + h/2 - y)^2)
        
        # To minimize distance, we want to center the rectangle around (x,y)
        # For distance minimization, we should try to make (x1 + w/2) as close to x as possible
        # and (y1 + h/2) as close to y as possible
        
        # Optimal x1: should be as close as possible to x - w/2
        x1_opt = x - w / 2
        x1_candidate = int(math.floor(x1_opt)) if x1_opt >= x1_min else x1_min
        x1_candidate = min(x1_candidate, x1_max)
        x1_candidate = max(x1_candidate, x1_min)
        
        x2 = x1_candidate + w
        
        # Optimal y1: should be as close as possible to y - h/2
        y1_opt = y - h / 2
        y1_candidate = int(math.floor(y1_opt)) if y1_opt >= y1_min else y1_min
        y1_candidate = min(y1_candidate, y1_max)
        y1_candidate = max(y1_candidate, y1_min)
        
        y2 = y1_candidate + h
        
        # Check if this rectangle contains (x,y)
        if x1_candidate <= x <= x2 and y1_candidate <= y <= y2:
            # Calculate distance squared
            dx = (x1_candidate + w / 2 - x)
            dy = (y1_candidate + h / 2 - y)
            distance_sq = dx * dx + dy * dy
            
            # Check if this is better
            if best_distance_sq == float('inf') or distance_sq < best_distance_sq:
                best_distance_sq = distance_sq
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_candidate, y1_candidate, x2, y2
            elif distance_sq == best_distance_sq:
                # If same distance, choose lexicographically smaller
                if (x1_candidate, y1_candidate) < (best_x1, best_y1):
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_candidate, y1_candidate, x2, y2
    
    # If no valid rectangle found, we should still have one (since k>=1 and constraints)
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

solve()
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the approach. The k range might be too large (up to 10^9). Let me optimize.

Actually, for large grids, we need to consider that only a few k values will give valid solutions. But the ranges are too big to iterate.

Let me think differently. For the rectangle to contain (x,y), we have constraints on k.

The maximum k is min(n//a, m//b). But this can be very large.

However, we only need to check values of k where the distance is minimized. The optimal solution will likely occur when the rectangle is centered.

For a given ratio a:b, I can parameterize the solutions.

```python
import math

n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Maximum k is bounded by n//a and m//b
max_k = min(n // a, m // b) if a > 0 and b > 0 else 0

best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
best_distance_sq = float('inf')

# We need to consider all possible k values
for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    w = k * a
    h = k * b
    
    # Check if we can fit this rectangle to contain (x,y)
    # x1 must be in [max(0, x - w), min(x, n - w)]
    x1_min = max(0, x - w)
    x1_max = min(x, n - w)
    
    # y1 must be in [max(0, y - h), min(y, m - h)]
    y1_min = max(0, y - h)
    y1_max = min(y, m - h)
    
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        continue
    
    # For this k, find the (x1, y1) that minimizes distance to center
    # Center should be as close to (x,y) as possible
    
    # Optimal x1: x - w/2 (center of the width)
    x1_opt = x - w / 2
    x1_val = int(round(x1_opt)) if x1_opt >= x1_min else x1_min
    x1_val = max(x1_val, x1_min)
    x1_val = min(x1_val, x1_max)
    
    # Optimal y1: y - h/2
    y1_opt = y - h / 2
    y1_val = int(round(y1_opt)) if y1_opt >= y1_min else y1_min
    y1_val = max(y1_val, y1_min)
    y1_val = min(y1_val, y1_max)
    
    # Make sure we're within bounds
    x1_val = max(x1_val, x1_min)
    x1_val = min(x1_val, x1_max)
    y1_val = max(y1_val, y1_min)
    y1_val = min(y1_val, y1_max)
    
    x2 = x1_val + w
    y2 = y1_val + h
    
    # Verify it contains (x,y)
    if x1_val <= x <= x2 and y1_val <= y <= y2:
        dx = (x1_val + w/2 - x)
        dy = (y1_val + h/2 - y)
        distance_sq = dx*dx + dy*dy
        
        if best_distance_sq == float('inf') or distance_sq < best_distance_sq:
            best_distance_sq = distance_sq
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_val, y1_val, x2, y2
        elif distance_sq == best_distance_sq:
            # Lexicographically smaller
            if (x1_val, y1_val) < (best_x1, best_y1):
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_val, y1_val, x2, y2

print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

View Extracted Code 
import math

n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Maximum k is bounded by n//a and m//b
max_k = min(n // a, m // b) if a > 0 and b > 0 else 0

best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
best_distance_sq = float('inf')

# We need to consider all possible k values
for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
    w = k * a
    h = k * b
    
    # Check if we can fit this rectangle to contain (x,y)
    # x1 must be in [max(0, x - w), min(x, n - w)]
    x1_min = max(0, x - w)
    x1_max = min(x, n - w)
    
    # y1 must be in [max(0, y - h), min(y, m - h)]
    y1_min = max(0, y - h)
    y1_max = min(y, m - h)
    
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        continue
    
    # For this k, find the (x1, y1) that minimizes distance to center
    # Center should be as close to (x,y) as possible
    
    # Optimal x1: x - w/2 (center of the width)
    x1_opt = x - w / 2
    x1_val = int(round(x1_opt)) if x1_opt >= x1_min else x1_min
    x1_val = max(x1_val, x1_min)
    x1_val = min(x1_val, x1_max)
    
    # Optimal y1: y - h/2
    y1_opt = y - h / 2
    y1_val = int(round(y1_opt)) if y1_opt >= y1_min else y1_min
    y1_val = max(y1_val, y1_min)
    y1_val = min(y1_val, y1_max)
    
    # Make sure we're within bounds
    x1_val = max(x1_val, x1_min)
    x1_val = min(x1_val, x1_max)
    y1_val = max(y1_val, y1_min)
    y1_val = min(y1_val, y1_max)
    
    x2 = x1_val + w
    y2 = y1_val + h
    
    # Verify it contains (x,y)
    if x1_val <= x <= x2 and y1_val <= y <= y2:
        dx = (x1_val + w/2 - x)
        dy = (y1_val + h/2 - y)
        distance_sq = dx*dx + dy*dy
        
        if best_distance_sq == float('inf') or distance_sq < best_distance_sq:
            best_distance_sq = distance_sq
            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_val, y1_val, x2, y2
        elif distance_sq == best_distance_sq:
            # Lexicographically smaller
            if (x1_val, y1_val) < (best_x1, best_y1):
                best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1_val, y1_val, x2, y2

print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

Input
100 100 52 50 46 56

Output
17 8 86 92### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates from (0,n) × (0,m)
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (where length = x₂-x₁, width = y₂-y₁)
   - Maximizes the area (or is as large as possible)
   - If multiple solutions, choose the one closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance
   - If still multiple, choose lexicographically smallest

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition: (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b
2. This means: b(x₂-x₁) = a(y₂-y₁)
3. So: x₂-x₁ = (a/b)(y₂-y₁)

## Mathematical Analysis

Let's define:
- w = x₂ - x₁ (width)
- h = y₂ - y₁ (height)

We need: w/h = a/b, so w = (a/b)h

Since w and h must be integers, h must be a multiple of b, and w must be a multiple of a.

Let h = k×b, then w = k×a for some positive integer k.

So we have a rectangle of width w = k×a and height h = k×b.

Constraints:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ → x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + w → x - w ≤ x₁ ≤ x
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ → y - h ≤ y₁ ≤ y
- Valid bounds: 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ n, 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ m

So we need:
- x₁ ≥ max(0, x - w) and x₁ ≤ min(x, n - w)
- y₁ ≥ max(0, y - h) and y₁ ≤ min(y, m - h)

The maximum possible k is limited by:
- k ≤ min((n - x)/a, (m - y)/b) for x₁ to be valid
- k ≤ min(x/a, (n - (x - x₁))/a) etc.

Actually, let me reconsider the bounds more carefully:

For x₁ to exist in the valid range:
- x₁ ≥ max(0, x - w) and x₁ ≤ min(x, n - w)
- So we need: max(0, x - w) ≤ min(x, n - w)

Which means we need:
- x - w ≤ n - w → x ≤ n (true)
- x - w ≤ x (always true)
- x - w ≤ n - w → x ≤ n (true)
- w ≤ n and w ≤ n (since x₁ ≤ n - w)

Actually, the constraint is that we need valid x₁ and x₂:
- x₁ ≥ max(0, x - w)
- x₁ ≤ min(x, n - w)

So we need: max(0, x - w) ≤ min(x, n - w)

This gives us:
- If x - w ≤ n - w → x ≤ n (always true)
- We need x - w ≤ x (always true)
- We need x - w ≤ n - w → x ≤ n (true)

The real constraint is that w ≤ n and h ≤ m, and the position constraints.

## Algorithm

1. Try different values of k (scaling factor)
2. For each k, calculate w = k×a, h = k×b
3. Check if w ≤ n and h ≤ m
4. Check if we can place the rectangle containing (x,y):
   - x₁ can be from max(0, x - w) to min(x, n - w)
   - y₁ can be from max(0, y - h) to min(y, m - h)
5. We want the maximum possible area, so maximize k
6. For the same k, we want to minimize Euclidean distance from center of rectangle to (x,y)
7. If still tied, choose lexicographically smallest

The center of the rectangle is at ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁+(x₁+w))/2, ((y₁+(y₁+h))/2) = (x₁ + w/2, y₁ + h/2)

For a fixed k, we want to minimize distance to (x,y):
- Distance² = (x₁ + w/2 - x)² + (y₁ + h/2 - y)²

But since x₁ and y₁ have constraints, we need to find optimal positions.

Actually, we can simplify: for a fixed k, we want to minimize the distance. The optimal center would be as close to (x,y) as possible.

But since we need integer coordinates, we need to check all valid (x₁,y₁) pairs for each k.

Wait, k can be very large (up to 10^9), so we can't iterate over all k.

## Optimized Approach

For maximum area, we want maximum k such that:
- k×a ≤ n and k×b ≤ m
- The rectangle can contain (x,y)

So max_k = min(floor(n/a), floor(m/b)) 

But we also need to ensure we can position the rectangle to contain (x,y).

For the rectangle of size w×h to contain (x,y):
- w ≤ n, h ≤ m (already satisfied)
- x₁ ≥ max(0, x - w) and x₁ ≤ min(x, n - w)
- So we need max(0, x - w) ≤ min(x, n - w)

This gives us constraints on w.

Actually, we need:
- max(0, x - w) ≤ min(x, n - w)

This means:
- x - w ≤ n - w → x ≤ n (always true)
- x - w ≤ x → -w ≤ 0 → w ≥ 0 (always true)
- x - w ≥ 0 → x ≥ w (only if x > 0)

The constraint is that x₁ must exist, so max(0, x - w) ≤ min(x, n - w)

This splits into cases:
1. If x - w ≤ 0: we need 0 ≤ min(x, n - w) → which is always true
2. If x - w > 0: we need x - w ≤ min(x, n - w)

But min(x, n - w) ≤ x, so we need x - w ≤ x → always true.
min(x, n - w) ≥ x - w → this gives us the constraint.

Actually, the constraint is simply that w ≤ n and w ≤ n (always true in our case).

The real constraint for x₁ to exist is that max(0, x - w) ≤ min(x, n - w).

This is always satisfied if w ≤ n and x ≥ 0.

Let me reconsider with the examples.

## Example Analysis

Example 1: n=9, m=9, x=5, y=5, a=2, b=1

Ratio 2:1, so w:h = 2:1.

Possible k values:
- k=1: w=2, h=1
- k=2: w=4, h=2  
- k=3: w=6, h=3
- k=4: w=8, h=4
- k=5: w=10, h=5 → w=10 > n=9 → invalid

So max k = 4.

For k=4: w=8, h=4
- x₁ in [max(0,5-8), min(5,9-8)] = [max(0,-3), min(5,1)] = [0,1]
- y₁ in [max(0,5-4), min(5,9-4)] = [max(0,1), min(5,5)] = [1,5]

Center for each (x₁,y₁):
- (x₁+4, y₁+2)

We want to minimize distance to (5,5):
- For (0,1): (4,3) → distance² = (5-4)² + (5-3)² = 1 + 4 = 5
- For (0,2): (4,4) → distance² = 1 + 1 = 2
- For (0,3): (4,5) → distance² = 1 + 0 = 1
- For (0,4): (4,6) → distance² = 1 + 1 = 2
- For (1,1): (5,3) → distance² = 0 + 4 = 4
- For (1,2): (5,4) → distance² = 0 + 1 = 1
- For (1,3): (5,5) → distance² = 0 + 0 = 0 ✅
- For (1,4): (5,6) → distance² = 0 + 1 = 1
- For (1,5): (5,7) → distance² = 0 + 4 = 4

Best is (1,3) with center (5,5), distance 0. This matches output.

For k=3: w=6, h=3
- x₁ in [max(0,5-6), min(5,9-6)] = [0,3]
- y₁ in [max(0,5-3), min(5,9-3)] = [2,6]
- Center at (x₁+3, y₁+1.5)

Distance to (5,5):
- Need to minimize, but will be at least some distance
- This has smaller area than k=4, so not optimal.

## Algorithm

1. Find maximum k such that k×a ≤ n and k×b ≤ m
2. For this k, find the best (x₁,y₁) to minimize Euclidean distance to (x,y)
3. Among those, choose lexicographically smallest if tie

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Maximum possible k such that k*a <= n and k*b <= m
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    # If no valid rectangle, but problem guarantees at least one (since a,b >= 1 and constraints)
    if max_k == 0:
        # This shouldn't happen given constraints
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    # Calculate width and height for maximum k
    w = a * max_k
    h = b * max_k
    
    # Determine valid ranges for x1 and y1
    # x1 must satisfy: max(0, x - w) <= x1 <= min(x, n - w)
    x1_min = max(0, x - w)
    x1_max = min(x, n - w)
    
    # y1 must satisfy: max(0, y - h) <= y1 <= min(y, m - h)
    y1_min = max(0, y - h)
    y1_max = min(y, m - h)
    
    # If no valid position, find a smaller k
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        # Try to find the maximum valid k
        k = max_k
        while k > 0:
            k -= 1
            w = a * k
            h = b * k
            
            x1_min = max(0, x - w)
            x1_max = min(x, n - w)
            y1_min = max(0, y - h)
            y1_max = min(y, m - h)
            
            if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
                break
        
        if k == 0:
            print(0, 0, 0, 0)
            return
    
    # Now find optimal (x1, y1) for this k
    best_x1 = x1_min
    best_y1 = y1_min
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # For each possible y1 in range
    for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
        # For each possible x1 in range
        for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
            # Calculate center
            center_x = x1 + w // 2
            center_y = y1 + h // 2
            
            # Calculate squared distance to (x, y)
            dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
            
            if dist_sq < best_dist_sq:
                best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                best_x1 = x1
                best_y1 = y1
            elif dist_sq == best_dist_sq:
                # Lexicographically smaller: prefer smaller x1, then smaller y1
                if x1 < best_x1 or (x1 == best_x1 and y1 < best_y1):
                    best_x1 = x1
                    best_y1 = y1
    
    # But the above loop might be too slow for large ranges
    # Let's optimize: for fixed y1, we want to minimize distance
    # The optimal x1 for a fixed y1 would be to make center_x as close to x as possible
    
    # Actually, for each y1, we can find the best x1
    best_x1 = x1_min
    best_y1 = y1_min
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # To avoid O(range) complexity, let's try to find optimal values mathematically
    # For fixed y1, center_y is fixed, so we want to minimize (center_x - x)²
    # center_x = x1 + w/2
    # So we want x1 as close as possible to x - w/2
    
    # But x1 must be in [x1_min, x1_max]
    for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
        # For this y1, the optimal x1 is as close as possible to x - w/2
        target_x1 = x - w / 2
        
        # Find x1 in [x1_min, x1_max] closest to target_x1
        x1_candidate = int(round(target_x1))
        
        # Check boundaries
        x1_candidates = []
        if x1_min <= x1_candidate <= x1_max:
            x1_candidates.append(x1_candidate)
        if x1_min <= x1_candidate - 1 <= x1_max:
            x1_candidates.append(x1_candidate - 1)
        if x1_min <= x1_candidate + 1 <= x1_max:
            x1_candidates.append(x1_candidate + 1)
        
        # Also include endpoints
        x1_candidates.extend([x1_min, x1_max])
        
        # Remove duplicates and sort
        x1_candidates = sorted(set(x1_candidates))
        
        for x1 in x1_candidates:
            if not (x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max):
                continue
                
            center_x = x1 + w // 2
            center_y = y1 + h // 2
            
            dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
            
            if dist_sq < best_dist_sq:
                best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                best_x1 = x1
                best_y1 = y1
            elif dist_sq == best_dist_sq:
                if x1 < best_x1 or (x1 == best_x1 and y1 < best_y1):
                    best_x1 = x1
                    best_y1 = y1
    
    x2 = x1 + w
    y2 = y1 + h
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, x2, y2)

solve()

Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b
   - Maximizes the area (or effectively the size)
   - If multiple solutions exist, choose the one closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance
   - If still tied, choose lexicographically minimum

## Key Insights

1. The ratio condition: (x₂ - x₁)/(y₂ - y₁) = a/b
2. This means: b(x₂ - x₁) = a(y₂ - y₁)
3. We can express this as: x₂ - x₁ = (a/b) × (y₂ - y₁)
4. Since we want integer coordinates, we need to ensure the dimensions are compatible

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Find possible widths and heights**: Since the ratio is a/b, the width must be a multiple of a and height must be a multiple of b, or we need to find the gcd to make it work with integers.

2. **Key insight**: The ratio condition means b×(width) = a×(height). So width/height = a/b, which means width×b = height×a.

3. **Find the required dimensions**: Let's say we want a width of w and height of h. Then we need w×b = h×a. This means w must be a multiple of a/gcd(a,b) and h must be a multiple of b/gcd(a,b).

4. **Maximize area**: Since we want maximum area, we should maximize both width and height, subject to:
   - 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ ≤ n
   - 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ ≤ m
   - x₂ - x₁ = w, y₂ - y₁ = h
   - w×b = h×a

5. **For each valid width-height pair**, check if it can contain (x,y) and select the best one.

## Mathematical Approach

Let g = gcd(a,b)
Then we can write: w = k×(a/g), h = k×(b/g) for some positive integer k

We want to maximize the area w×h = k²×(a×b/g²)

Constraints:
- w ≤ n (width constraint) → k×(a/g) ≤ n → k ≤ n×g/a
- h ≤ m (height constraint) → k×(b/g) ≤ m → k ≤ m×g/b
- The rectangle must contain (x,y)

For a fixed k, the maximum width is w_max = k×(a/g) and height h_max = k×(b/g)

To contain (x,y), we need:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ → x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + w → x - w ≤ x₁ ≤ x
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ → y - h ≤ y₁ ≤ y

So x₁ ∈ [max(0, x - w), min(x, n - w)] and y₁ ∈ [max(0, y - h), min(y, m - h)]

For the maximum size, we take the largest possible k.

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Find gcd of a and b
    g = math.gcd(a, b)
    
    # The ratio condition: w * b = h * a
    # So w = k * (a/g), h = k * (b/g)
    # We want to maximize k
    
    # Maximum k from width constraint
    k_max_width = n // (a // g) if a // g != 0 else float('inf')
    
    # Maximum k from height constraint
    k_max_height = m // (b // g) if b // g != 0 else float('inf')
    
    # Maximum k is the minimum of both
    k_max = min(k_max_width, k_max_height)
    
    # If k_max is 0, we can't have any valid rectangle (shouldn't happen with constraints)
    if k_max <= 0:
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    # Use maximum k to get dimensions
    width = k_max * (a // g)
    height = k_max * (b // g)
    
    # Now find the best position to place this rectangle containing (x,y)
    # x1, x2 such that x1 <= x <= x1 + width
    # So x1 >= x - width and x1 <= x
    # Also x1 >= 0 and x1 <= n - width
    
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    
    # If no valid x1 range, we need to reduce k
    if x1_min > x1_max:
        # Reduce k until we find a valid solution
        k = k_max
        while k > 0:
            k -= 1
            width = k * (a // g)
            height = k * (b // g)
            x1_min = max(0, x - width)
            x1_max = min(x, n - width)
            if x1_min <= x1_max:
                break
        if k == 0:
            print(0, 0, 0, 0)
            return
    
    # y1, y2 such that y1 <= y <= y1 + height
    # So y1 >= y - height and y1 <= y
    # Also y1 >= 0 and y1 <= m - height
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)
    
    if y1_min > y1_max:
        # This shouldn't happen with valid k, but just in case
        k = k_max
        while k > 0:
            k -= 1
            width = k * (a // g)
            height = k * (b // g)
            y1_min = max(0, y - height)
            y1_max = min(y, m - height)
            if y1_min <= y1_max:
                break
        if k == 0:
            print(0, 0, 0, 0)
            return
    
    # Now we have valid ranges for x1 and y1
    # We need to find the position that minimizes Euclidean distance to (x,y)
    # Center of rectangle is ((x1+x2)/2, (y1+y2)/2)
    # x2 = x1 + width, y2 = y1 + height
    # Distance squared = ((x1 + width)/2 - x)² + ((y1 + height)/2 - y)²
    
    # Since we want to minimize distance, and x1 in [x1_min, x1_max], y1 in [y1_min, y1_max]
    # We can try to find optimal values.
    
    # For distance minimization, the center should be as close to (x,y) as possible.
    # The optimal x1 would be x - width/2 if possible, otherwise the boundary.
    # Similarly for y1.
    
    # Find best x1 and y1
    best_x1 = x1_min
    best_y1 = y1_min
    
    # Check if we can place center closer to (x,y)
    x_center = x - width / 2
    y_center = y - height / 2
    
    # Try to move x1 and y1 to minimize distance
    # x1 should be as close as possible to (x - width/2)
    target_x1 = x - width / 2
    if target_x1 >= x1_min and target_x1 <= x1_max:
        best_x1 = int(target_x1)
    else:
        if target_x1 < x1_min:
            best_x1 = x1_min
        elif target_x1 > x1_max:
            best_x1 = x1_max
    
    # Similarly for y1
    target_y1 = y - height / 2
    if target_y1 >= y1_min and target_y1 <= y1_max:
        best_y1 = int(target_y1)
    else:
        if target_y1 < y1_min:
            best_y1 = y1_min
        elif target_y1 > y1_max:
            best_y1 = y1_max
    
    # Ensure we have integer coordinates
    x1 = best_x1
    y1 = best_y1
    x2 = x1 + width
    y2 = y1 + height
    
    # If there are ties, we need lexicographically minimum
    # But what we have should be optimal for distance and should be lexicographically good
    
    print(x1, y1, x2, y2)

solve()

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Find gcd of a and b
    g = math.gcd(a, b)
    
    # The ratio condition: width * b = height * a
    # So width = k * (a//g), height = k * (b//g)
    
    # Maximum k value
    k_width_max = n // (a // g) if a // g > 0 else 0
    k_height_max = m // (b // g) if b // g > 0 else 0
    k_max = min(k_width_max, k_height_max)
    
    if k_max <= 0:
        # Shouldn't happen with given constraints
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    # Use maximum k
    width = k_max * (a // g)
    height = k_max * (b // g)
    
    # Find valid x1 and y1 ranges
    # x1 <= x <= x1 + width → x1 >= x - width and x1 <= x
    # x1 >= 0 and x1 <= n - width
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    
    # y1 <= y <= y1 + height → y1 >= y - height and y1 <= y  
    # y1 >= 0 and y1 <= m - height
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)
    
    # If no valid position, reduce k
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        # Reduce k until valid position found
        k = k_max
        while k > 0:
            k -= 1
            w = k * (a // g)
            h = k * (b // g)
            if w > n or h > m:
                continue
            x1_min = max(0, x - w)
            x1_max = min(x, n - w)
            y1_min = max(0, y - h)
            y1_max = min(y, m - h)
            if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
                width = w
                height = h
                break
        if k == 0:
            print(0, 0, 0, 0)
            return
    
    # Now find optimal position to minimize Euclidean distance from center to (x,y)
    # Center is at (x1 + width/2, y1 + height/2)
    # Distance squared = (x1 + width/2 - x)² + (y1 + height/2 - y)²
    
    # We want to minimize this distance
    # The ideal x1 would be x - width/2
    ideal_x1 = x - width / 2
    ideal_y1 = y - height / 2
    
    # Choose x1 to minimize distance
    x1 = x1_min
    if ideal_x1 >= x1_min and ideal_x1 <= x1_max:
        x1 = int(ideal_x1)
    else:
        if ideal_x1 < x1_min:
            x1 = x1_min
        else:
            x1 = x1_max
    
    # Choose y1 to minimize distance
    y1 = y1_min
    if ideal_y1 >= y1_min and ideal_y1 <= y1_max:
        y1 = int(ideal_y1)
    else:
        if ideal_y1 < y1_min:
            y1 = y1_min
        else:
            y1 = y1_max
    
    x2 = x1 + width
    y2 = y1 + height
    
    print(x1, y1, x2, y2)

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image]

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the given point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a:b (i.e., (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b)
   - Is as close as possible to the point (x,y) in terms of Euclidean distance to the center
   - If multiple solutions, choose lexicographically minimum one

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Key Insights

The ratio condition (x₂-x₁)/(y₂-y₁) = a/b means:
- x₂ - x₁ = a * k
- y₂ - y₁ = b * k
for some positive integer k.

So we need to find values of k such that:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ = x₁ + a*k
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ = y₁ + b*k
- All coordinates are within bounds

### Step 2: Constraints Analysis

For the x-direction:
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₁ + a*k
- So x₁ ≤ x and x₁ ≥ x - a*k
- Also x₁ ≥ 0 and x₁ + a*k ≤ n, so x₁ ≤ n - a*k

For the y-direction:
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₁ + b*k
- So y₁ ≤ y and y₁ ≥ y - b*k
- Also y₁ ≥ 0 and y₁ + b*k ≤ m, so y₁ ≤ m - b*k

So x₁ = max(0, x - a*k, x - a*k/2) but capped at min(x, n - a*k)

Wait, this is getting complex. Let me simplify.

For each k, the valid range for x₁ is [lower_x, upper_x] where:
- lower_x = max(0, x - a*k)
- upper_x = min(x, n - a*k)

Similarly for y₁: [lower_y, upper_y] where:
- lower_y = max(0, y - b*k)
- upper_y = min(y, m - b*k)

To minimize distance from center to (x,y), we want the center to be as close as possible to (x,y).

Center x = x₁ + a*k/2, so we want x₁ as close as possible to x - a*k/2.

So choose x₁ = max(lower_x, x - a*k/2) for the minimum distance, but also ensure it's ≤ upper_x.

Similarly for y₁.

So optimal x₁ = max(lower_x, x - a*k/2) if max(lower_x, x - a*k/2) ≤ upper_x, otherwise we need to adjust.

Actually, if max(lower_x, x - a*k/2) > upper_x, then we have to choose x₁ = upper_x, which gives a center at upper_x + a*k/2.

But this might not be optimal. However, since we're comparing distances, we need to consider all possible k values.

But k can be very large (up to 10^9), so we can't iterate over all k.

### Step 5: Optimizing k Search

The valid k values are from 0 to min(n//a, m//b).

But we don't want to iterate over all possible k values. We need to find which k gives the best result.

The distance function is not monotonic, but we can analyze it.

### Step 6: Mathematical Analysis

The distance to center is: ((x₁ + a*k/2) - x)² + ((y₁ + b*k/2) - y)²

But x₁ and y₁ are constrained.

The optimal choice for x₁ given k is:
- x₁ = max(0, x - a*k), x - a*k/2) but within [max(0, x - a*k), min(x, n - a*k)]

So x₁_opt = max(0, x - a*k, x - a*k/2) if this is ≤ min(x, n - a*k), otherwise we have to use boundary value.

Actually, since x - a*k/2 > x - a*k, and we want to minimize distance, we should choose x₁ as close as possible to x - a*k/2.

So x₁ = max(0, x - a*k, x - a*k/2) if it's ≤ min(x, n - a*k), else if x - a*k/2 > min(x, n - a*k), then x₁ = min(x, n - a*k)

Similarly for y₁.

But if x - a*k/2 > min(x, n - a*k), then we have to take the upper bound.

For distance minimization, we want the center as close as possible to (x,y).

So for each k, the center is at (x₁ + a*k/2, y₁ + b*k/2) where:
- x₁ + a*k/2 ≥ x - a*k/2 + a*k/2 = x (if x₁ ≥ x - a*k)
- x₁ + a*k/2 ≤ x + a*k/2

The distance is minimized when x₁ + a*k/2 is as close as possible to x, and similarly for y.

So for a given k, optimal center x_coord = max(x - a*k/2, some constant) but within bounds.

I think the best approach is to realize that k will be in a reasonable range, and we can iterate over possible k values.

But the range is too large.

Wait, maybe we only need to check k values that are "critical" - when the boundaries are reached.

The critical k values are when:
- x - a*k = 0 → k = x/a
- x - a*k = x → k = 0
- x - a*k = n - a*k → x = n
- etc.

Actually, the optimal k is likely near the values where the center would be closest.

But to be safe, let me try a different approach.

Since a and b are given, and we need the ratio a:b, the length and width are a*k and b*k for some positive integer k.

For each k, we determine if a valid rectangle exists, and compute the center distance.

The valid k values are from 0 to min(n//a, m//b).

If min(n//a, m//b) is very large, we can't iterate.

But in practice, the optimal k might not be at the extremes.

However, let's look at the examples:

Example 1: n=9, m=9, x=5, y=5, a=2, b=1
We need (x2-x1)/ (y2-y1) = 2/1

k can be 1,2,3,4 (since a*k ≤ 9, b*k ≤ 9, so k ≤ 9//2 = 4, and k ≤ 9//1 = 9)

For k=1: width=2, height=1
x1 ∈ [max(0,5-2), min(5,9-2)] = [3,3]
y1 ∈ [max(0,5-1), min(5,9-1)] = [4,4]
So x1=3, y1=4, rectangle (3,4,5,5)
Center at (4,4.5), distance to (5,5): sqrt((1)^2 + (0.5)^2) = sqrt(1.25)

For k=2: width=4, height=2
x1 ∈ [max(0,5-4), min(5,9-4)] = [1,1]
y1 ∈ [max(0,5-2), min(5,9-2)] = [3,3]
So x1=1, y1=3, rectangle (1,3,5,7)
Center at (3,5), distance to (5,5): sqrt(4^2 + 0^2) = 4

k=3: width=6, height=3
x1 ∈ [max(0,5-6), min(5,9-6)] = [0,3]
y1 ∈ [max(0,5-3), min(5,9-3)] = [2,2]
x1 should be close to 5 - 3*2 = -1, so we want x1 as small as possible? 
Center x = x1 + 3, want this close to 5.
So x1 + 3 ≈ 5 → x1 ≈ 2
x1 ∈ [0,3], so x1=2
y1 = 2
Rectangle (2,2,8,5)
Center (5,3.5), distance to (5,5): sqrt(0^2 + 1.5^2) = 1.5

k=4: width=8, height=4
x1 ∈ [max(0,5-8), min(5,9-8)] = [0,1]
y1 ∈ [max(0,5-4), min(5,9-4)] = [1,1]
Center x = x1 + 4, want close to 5
x1 + 4 ≈ 5 → x1 ≈ 1
x1 ∈ [0,1], so x1=1
y1=1
Rectangle (1,1,9,5)
Center (5,3), distance to (5,5): sqrt(0^2 + 2^2) = 2

So k=3 gives the minimum distance of 1.5.

But the expected output is (1,3,9,7), which is for k=4: x2-x1=8, y2-y1=4, so a/b=2/1.

(1,3,9,7): x1=1,y1=3,x2=9,y2=7
Contains (5,5)? 1≤5≤9, 3≤5≤7 → yes
Ratio: (8)/(4) = 2/1 → yes

Center at (5,5), distance 0! 

Oh! The center is at (5,5). So for (1,3,9,7), center is ((1+9)/2, (3+7)/2) = (5,5).

So distance is 0, which is minimum possible.

How is this possible? For k=4, with x1=1, y1=3:
x1 ≥ max(0,5-8)=0, ≤ min(5,9-8)=1 → so x1=1 is valid
y1 ≥ max(0,5-4)=1, ≤ min(5,9-4)=5 → y1=3 is valid

And center is (5,5).

So for k=4, we can achieve center exactly at (5,5).

For other k values, we couldn't get center at (5,5).

So the key is to find k such that we can make the center at (x,y), or very close.

I think I need to check for each possible k whether the center can be at (x,y), and if not, find the closest.

But with large ranges, this is not feasible.

However, in the problem, we might not need to check all k. We only need to check when the center can be at (x,y).

For the center to be at (x,y):
- (x1 + (a*k)/2) = x → x1 = x - (a*k)/2
- (y1 + (b*k)/2) = y → y1 = y - (b*k)/2

We need:
- x1 ≥ 0 and x1 ≤ n - a*k
- y1 ≥ 0 and y1 ≤ m - b*k

So:
- x - (a*k)/2 ≥ 0 → k ≤ (2*x)/a
- x - (a*k)/2 ≤ n - a*k → x - (a*k)/2 ≤ n - a*k → x - n ≤ - (a*k)/2 → k ≥ 2*(n-x)/a
- y - (b*k)/2 ≥ 0 → k ≤ 2*y/b
- y - (b*k)/2 ≤ m - b*k → y - (b*k)/2 ≤ m - b*k → y - m ≤ - (b*k)/2 → k ≥ 2*(m-y)/b

So for the center to be exactly at (x,y), we need:
- k ≤ min(2*x/a, 2*y/b)
- k ≥ max(2*(n-x)/a, 2*(m-y)/b)

And k must be such that a*k ≤ n, b*k ≤ m.

If there is a valid integer k in this range, then we have a solution with distance 0.

If not, we need to find the k that minimizes the distance.

But the range might be very large, so we need to find if there's any k in [max(2*(n-x)/a, 2*(m-y)/b), min(2*x/a, 2*y/b)].

If there is, we can find the smallest such k (for lexicographic order).

Otherwise, we need to find the k that minimizes the distance.

Given the complexity and the constraints, I'll implement a solution that:

1. Checks if there exists k such that center is at (x,y)
2. If not, finds the k that minimizes the distance (by checking critical values)

But for large inputs, we need to be careful.

After more thought, I believe the intended solution is to check for k values that make the center close to (x,y), and since the distance is quadratic, the minimum is likely near the values where the bounds are tight.

Given the time, I'll implement a solution that checks a range of k values around the critical points.

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # We need to find k such that a*k <= n, b*k <= m
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    # If max_k is 0, then no valid rectangle
    if max_k == 0:
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = None, None, None, None
    best_dist_squared = float('inf')
    
    # We need to find all possible k values
    # But k can be up to 10^9, so we need to be smart
    
    # Check if we can have center exactly at (x, y)
    # This requires:
    # x1 = x - (a*k)/2, y1 = y - (b*k)/2
    # with 0 <= x1 <= n - a*k, 0 <= y1 <= m - b*k
    
    # Conditions:
    # x - (a*k)/2 >= 0  => k <= 2*x/a
    # x - (a*k)/2 <= n - a*k  => x - (a*k)/2 <= n - a*k => x - n <= - (a*k)/2 => k >= 2*(n-x)/a
    # y - (b*k)/2 >= 0  => k <= 2*y/b
    # y - (b*k)/2 <= m - b*k  => y - (b*k)/2 <= m - b*k => y - m <= - (b*k)/2 => k >= 2*(m-y)/b
    
    # So k must satisfy: k >= max(0, 2*(n-x)/a, 2*(m-y)/b) and k <= min(2*x/a, 2*y/b)
    
    min_k_center = max(0, (2 * (n - x)) / a, (2 * (m - y)) / b)
    max_k_center = min((2 * x) / a, (2 * y) / b)
    
    if min_k_center <= max_k_center:
        # There exists k such that center is at (x,y)
        # We want the lexicographically minimum solution, so we try smallest k
        k = int(math.ceil(min_k_center))
        while k <= max_k_center and k <= max_k:
            # Check if this k gives valid rectangle with center at (x,y)
            x1_center = x - (a * k) / 2
            y1_center = y - (b * k) / 2
            
            # Check if x1_center and y1_center are within bounds
            if x1_center >= 0 and x1_center <= n - a*k and y1_center >= 0 and y1_center <= m - b*k:
                # We need integer coordinates
                # x1 should be floor or ceil of x1_center to be close
                # But we want the center to be as close as possible
                # For minimum distance with this k, the best is to have center exactly at (x,y)
                # So we need to find integer x1, y1 such that center is (x,y)
                # x1 = x - a*k/2, so it may not be integer
                # We can only have exact center at (x,y) if x - a*k/2 and y - b*k/2 are integers
                if (a * k) % 2 == 0 and (b * k) % 2 == 0:
                    x1 = x - (a * k) // 2
                    y1 = y - (b * k) // 2
                    if x1 >= 0 and x1 <= n - a*k and y1 >= 0 and y1 <= m - b*k:
                        x2 = x1 + a*k
                        y2 = y1 + b*k
                        dist_sq = 0
                        if best_dist_squared > dist_sq or (best_dist_squared == dist_sq and (x1, y1, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)):
                            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                            best_dist_squared = dist_sq
                else:
                    # Need to find closest center
                    k_val = k
                    # Try to find best x1, y1 for this k
                    x1_opts = []
                    y1_opts = []
                    
                    # x1 in [max(0, x - a*k), min(x, n - a*k)]
                    low_x = max(0, x - a * k)
                    high_x = min(x, n - a * k)
                    if low_x <= high_x:
                        x1_opts = [low_x, high_x]  # Try boundaries
                        # Try values close to optimal
                        opt_x1 = x - (a * k) / 2
                        if opt_x1 >= low_x and opt_x1 <= high_x:
                            x1_opts.append(int(round(opt_x1)))
                    
                    # y1 in [max(0, y - b*k), min(y, m - b*k)]
                    low_y = max(0, y - b * k)
                    high_y = min(y, m - b * k)
                    if low_y <= high_y:
                        y1_opts = [low_y, high_y]
                        opt_y1 = y - (b * k) / 2
                        if opt_y1 >= low_y and opt_y1 <= high_y:
                            y1_opts.append(int(round(opt_y1)))
                    
                    for x1 in x1_opts:
                        for y1 in y1_opts:
                            x2 = x1 + a * k
                            y2 = y1 + b * k
                            if x1 >= 0 and x1 <= n - a*k and y1 >= 0 and y1 <= m - b*k:
                                center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2
                                center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2
                                dist_sq = (center_x - x)**2 + (center_y - y)**2
                                if dist_sq < best_dist_squared or (dist_sq == best_dist_squared and (x1, y1, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)):
                                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                                    best_dist_squared = dist_sq
            k += 1
    else:
        # No k gives center exactly at (x,y)
        # We need to find k that minimizes distance
        # Try k values around the critical points
        # The distance function might have minimum near where the bounds are tight
        # Try k from 0 to max_k
        for k in range(0, max_k + 1):
            # Find best x1, y1 for this k
            low_x = max(0, x - a * k)
            high_x = min(x, n - a * k)
            low_y = max(0, y - b * k)
            high_y = min(y, m - b * k)
            
            if low_x > high_x or low_y > high_y:
                continue
                
            # Try boundaries and optimal values
            x1_opts = [low_x, high_x]
            opt_x1 = x - (a * k) / 2
            if opt_x1 >= low_x and opt_x1 <= high_x:
                x1_opts.append(int(round(opt_x1)))
                
            y1_opts = [low_y, high_y]
            opt_y1 = y - (b * k) / 2
            if opt_y1 >= low_y and opt_y1 <= high_y:
                y1_opts.append(int(round(opt_y1)))
            
            for x1 in x1_opts:
                for y1 in y1_opts:
                    x2 = x1 + a * k
                    y2 = y1 + b * k
                    if x1 >= 0 and x1 <= n - a*k and y1 >= 0 and y1 <= m - b*k:
                        center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2
                        center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2
                        dist_sq = (center_x - x)**2 + (center_y - y)**2
                        if dist_sq < best_dist_squared or (dist_sq == best_dist_squared and (x1, y1, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)):
                            best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                            best_dist_squared = dist_sq
    
    if best_x1 is None:
        # This should not happen if max_k > 0
        best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = 0, 0, 0, 0
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)

solve()
```

Wait, this might still be too slow for large inputs. Let me reconsider the problem.

Looking at the examples more carefully, in example 1, the output is (1,3,9,7), which has center (5,5). So the distance is 0.

In example 2, the output is (17,8,86,92).

The only way to achieve distance 0 is if (x1+x2)/2 = x and (y1+y2)/2 = y.

This gives x1 + x2 = 2x and y1 + y2 = 2y.

With x2 - x1 = a*k, y2 - y1 = b*k.

So from x2 - x1 = a*k and x1 + x2 = 2x, we get:
x1 = x - a*k/2, x2 = x + a*k/2

Similarly, y1 = y - b*k/2, y2 = y + b*k/2

Constraints:
- x1 >= 0 → x - a*k/2 >= 0 → k <= 2*x/a
- x2 <= n → x + a*k/2 <= n → k <= 2*(n-x)/a
- y1 >= 0 → y - b*k/2 >= 0 → k <= 2*y/b
- y2 <= m → y + b*k/2 <= m → k <= 2*(m-y)/b

So k <= min(2*x/a, 2*(n-x)/a, 2*y/b, 2*(m-y)/b)

Also from width and height: a*k <= n, b*k <= m.

For the center to be at (x,y), we need:
k <= min(2*x/a, 2*(n-x)/a, 2*y/b, 2*(m-y)/b)

And k >= 0.

If this interval is non-empty, then there exists k.

We want the lexicographically smallest solution, so we want the smallest possible k that satisfies the constraints.

But also, we need x1 = x - a*k/2 >= 0 and x1 <= n - a*k, etc.

The conditions are:
- x - a*k/2 >= 0 → k <= 2*x/a
- x + a*k/2 <= n → k <= 2*(n-x)/a
- y - b*k/2 >= 0 → k <= 2*y/b
- y + b*k/2 <= m → k <= 2*(m-y)/b

So k <= min(2*x/a, 2*(n-x)/a, 2*y/b, 2*(m-y)/b)

Since we want integer k, and we want lexicographically minimum, we can try the smallest k that satisfies the above, and also satisfies a*k <= n, b*k <= m.

But we also need k >= 0.

So the valid k values are from 0 to min( floor( min(2*x/a, 2*(n-x)/a, 2*y/b, 2*(m-y)/b) ), floor( min(n/a, m/b) ) )

We can try k from 0 upwards until we find one.

But it might be that no k makes the center at (x,y), then we need to find k that minimizes the distance.

Given the complexity and the fact that the examples show distance 0 is achievable, I'll implement accordingly.

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# If no valid rectangle
if a > n or b > m:
    print(0, 0, 0, 0)
else:
    # Try to find k such that center is at (x,y)
    # We need: x1 = x - a*k/2, x2 = x + a*k/2, etc.
    # So k must satisfy:
    # k <= 2*x/a
    # k <= 2*(n-x)/a
    # k <= 2*y/b
    # k <= 2*(m-y)/b
    # k >= 0
    
    max_k_center = min(2*x//a, 2*(n-x)//a, 2*y//b, 2*(m-y)//b)
    max_k_width_height = min(n//a, m//b)
    
    k = 0
    while k <= max_k_center and k <= max_k_width_height:
        # Check if we can have center at (x,y)
        # x1 = x - a*k/2, x2 = x + a*k/2
        # For this to be integer, if a*k is even then x1 is integer
        # For center at (x,y), we need x1 and x2 to have integer x1
        # Similarly for y
        
        # x1 = x - a*k/2, so a*k must be even for x1 to be integer
        # x2 = x + a*k/2, similarly
        # So a*k must be even and b*k must be even
        
        if (a * k) % 2 == 0 and (b * k) % 2 == 0:
            x1 = x - (a * k) // 2
            y1 = y - (b * k) // 2
            x2 = x1 + a * k
            y2 = y1 + b * k
            
            if 0 <= x1 <= n - a*k and 0 <= y1 <= m - b*k:
                print(x1, y1, x2, y2)
                exit(0)
        k += 1
    
    # If no such k, try to find the best rectangle with minimum distance
    # We'll try k from 0 to min(n//a, m//b)
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = 0, 0, 0, 0
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # To avoid TLE, we only try critical k values
    # Try k=0, and k=1,2,3,... up to a reasonable limit
    # But if max_k is large, we need to be careful
    
    max_k = min(n//a, m//b)
    for k in range(0, min(max_k + 1, 1000)):  # Try only first 1000 values
        low_x = max(0, x - a*k)
        high_x = min(x, n - a*k)
        low_y = max(0, y - b*k)
        high_y = min(y, m - b*k)
        
        if low_x > high_x or low_y > high_y:
            continue
            
        # Try optimal values for x1 and y1
        x1_candidate = int(round(x - (a*k)/2))
        y1_candidate = int(round(y - (b*k)/2))
        
        # Try boundaries and candidate
        candidates = []
        if low_x <= high_x:
            candidates.extend([low_x, high_x])
            if low_x <= x1_candidate <= high_x:
                candidates.append(x1_candidate)
        if low_y <= high_y:
            candidates.extend([low_y, high_y])
            if low_y <= y1_candidate <= high_y:
                candidates.append(y1_candidate)
        
        for x1 in candidates:
            for y1 in candidates:
                if x1 < 0 or x1 > n - a*k or y1 < 0 or y1 > m - b*k:
                    continue
                x2 = x1 + a*k
                y2 = y1 + b*k
                center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2
                center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2
                dist_sq = (center_x - x)**2 + (center_y - y)**2
                if dist_sq < best_dist_sq or (dist_sq == best_dist_sq and (x1, y1, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)):
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                    best_dist_sq = dist_sq
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
```

View Extracted Code 
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# If no valid rectangle
if a > n or b > m:
    print(0, 0, 0, 0)
else:

    # We need: x1 = x - a*k/2, x2 = x + a*k/2, etc.

    # k <= 2*y/b
    # k <= 2*(m-y)/b
    # k >= 0
    
    max_k_center = min(2*x//a, 2*(n-x)//a, 2*y//b, 2*(m-y)//b)
    max_k_width_height = min(n//a, m//b)
    
    k = 0
    while k <= max_k_center and k <= max_k_width_height:
        # Check if we can have center at (x,y)
        # x1 = x - a*k/2, x2 = x + a*k/2
        # For this to be integer, if a*k is even then x1 is integer
        # For center at (x,y), we need x1 and x2 to have integer x1
        # Similarly for y
        
        # x1 = x - a*k/2, so a*k must be even for x1 to be integer
        # x2 = x + a*k/2, similarly
        # So a*k must be even and b*k must be even
        
        if (a * k) % 2 == 0 and (b * k) % 2 == 0:
            x1 = x - (a * k) // 2
            y1 = y - (b * k) // 2
            x2 = x1 + a * k
            y2 = y1 + b * k
            
            if 0 <= x1 <= n - a*k and 0 <= y1 <= m - b*k:
                print(x1, y1, x2, y2)
                exit(0)
        k += 1
    
    # If no such k, try to find the best rectangle with minimum distance
    # We'll try k from 0 to min(n//a, m//b)
    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = 0, 0, 0, 0
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # To avoid TLE, we only try critical k values
    # Try k=0, and k=1,2,3,... up to a reasonable limit
    # But if max_k is large, we need to be careful
    
    max_k = min(n//a, m//b)
    for k in range(0, min(max_k + 1, 1000)):  # Try only first 1000 values
        low_x = max(0, x - a*k)
        high_x = min(x, n - a*k)
        low_y = max(0, y - b*k)
        high_y = min(y, m - b*k)
        
        if low_x > high_x or low_y > high_y:
            continue
            
        # Try optimal values for x1 and y1
        x1_candidate = int(round(x - (a*k)/2))
        y1_candidate = int(round(y - (b*k)/2))
        
        # Try boundaries and candidate
        candidates = []
        if low_x <= high_x:
            candidates.extend([low_x, high_x])
            if low_x <= x1_candidate <= high_x:
                candidates.append(x1_candidate)
        if low_y <= high_y:
            candidates.extend([low_y, high_y])
            if low_y <= y1_candidate <= high_y:
                candidates.append(y1_candidate)
        
        for x1 in candidates:
            for y1 in candidates:
                if x1 < 0 or x1 > n - a*k or y1 < 0 or y1 > m - b*k:
                    continue
                x2 = x1 + a*k
                y2 = y1 + b*k
                center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2
                center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2
                dist_sq = (center_x - x)**2 + (center_y - y)**2
                if dist_sq < best_dist_sq or (dist_sq == best_dist_sq and (x1, y1, x2, y2) < (best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)):
                    best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2 = x1, y1, x2, y2
                    best_dist_sq = dist_sq
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x2, best_y2)
