Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given a rectangle grid. That grid's size is n × m. Let's denote the coordinate system on the grid. So, each point on the grid will have coordinates — a pair of integers (x, y) (0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m).

Your task is to find a maximum sub-rectangle on the grid (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2) so that it contains the given point (x, y), and its length-width ratio is exactly (a, b). In other words the following conditions must hold: 0 ≤ x_1 ≤ x ≤ x_2 ≤ n, 0 ≤ y_1 ≤ y ≤ y_2 ≤ m, $\frac{x_{2} - x_{1}}{y_{2} - y_{1}} = \frac{a}{b}$.

The sides of this sub-rectangle should be parallel to the axes. And values x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 should be integers. [Image] 

If there are multiple solutions, find the rectangle which is closest to (x, y). Here "closest" means the Euclid distance between (x, y) and the center of the rectangle is as small as possible. If there are still multiple solutions, find the lexicographically minimum one. Here "lexicographically minimum" means that we should consider the sub-rectangle as sequence of integers (x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2), so we can choose the lexicographically minimum one.


-----Input-----

The first line contains six integers n, m, x, y, a, b (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^9, 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m, 1 ≤ a ≤ n, 1 ≤ b ≤ m).


-----Output-----

Print four integers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2, which represent the founded sub-rectangle whose left-bottom point is (x_1, y_1) and right-up point is (x_2, y_2).


-----Examples-----
Input
9 9 5 5 2 1

Output
1 3 9 7

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates (x,y) where 0 ≤ x ≤ n, 0 ≤ y ≤ m
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains the point (x,y)
   - Has length-width ratio exactly a/b
   - Maximizes the area (which means maximizing both dimensions)
   - If multiple solutions, chooses the one closest to (x,y) in Euclidean distance
   - If still tied, chooses lexicographically smallest

## Key Insights

1. For a rectangle with width w and height h, we need w/h = a/b, so w = k*a and h = k*b for some positive integer k
2. The width constraint: x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ ≤ n, so x₂ - x₁ = w
3. The height constraint: y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ ≤ m, so y₂ - y₁ = h
4. We want to maximize the area w×h, so we maximize k (since w = k*a, h = k*b)

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Find maximum possible k**: 
   - Width constraint: w = k*a ≤ n - x₁ + x₂, but x₂ - x₁ = k*a
   - Since x₁ ≥ 0 and x₂ ≤ n, we need k*a ≤ n
   - Similarly, k*b ≤ m
   - So maximum k is min(n//a, m//b)

2. **For maximum k, find valid positions**:
   - Width: x₂ - x₁ = k*a, with x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂
   - Height: y₂ - y₁ = k*b, with y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂
   - We need to find valid (x₁, x₂) pairs such that x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ and x₂ - x₁ = k*a
   - Similarly for y coordinates

3. **Finding valid ranges**:
   - For x coordinates: x₁ can range from max(0, x - k*a) to min(x, n - k*a)
   - For y coordinates: y₁ can range from max(0, y - k*b) to min(y, m - k*b)

4. **Calculate center distance and find optimal solution**:
   - Center of rectangle: ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁ + x₁ + k*a)/2, (y₁ + y₁ + k*b)/2)
   - Distance to (x,y): √[((x₁ + k*a/2) - x)² + (((y₁ + k*b/2) - y)²]
   - We want to minimize this distance

5. **Lexicographic ordering**: If distances are equal, choose lexicographically smallest (x₁, y₁)

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Find maximum possible k such that k*a <= n and k*b <= m
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    if max_k == 0:
        # No valid rectangle possible
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    k = max_k
    width = k * a
    height = k * b
    
    # Find valid x1 range: x1 <= x <= x1 + width
    # So x1 <= x and x1 + width >= x
    # Therefore: x - width <= x1 <= x
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    
    # Find valid y1 range: y1 <= y <= y1 + height
    # So y - height <= y1 <= y
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)
    
    # If no valid positions, try smaller k
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        # We need to find the largest k such that valid positions exist
        # Try smaller k values
        for k in range(max_k, 0, -1):
            width = k * a
            height = k * b
            
            x1_min = max(0, x - width)
            x1_max = min(x, n - width)
            
            y1_min = max(0, y - height)
            y1_max = min(y, m - height)
            
            if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
                break
        
        if k == 0:
            print(0, 0, 0, 0)
            return
    
    # Now we have valid k, find the optimal (x1, y1)
    best_x1 = x1_min
    best_y1 = y1_min
    best_distance_squared = float('inf')
    
    # For each possible x1 in valid range
    for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
        x2 = x1 + width
        
        # For each possible y1 in valid range
        for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
            y2 = y1 + height
            
            # Calculate center of rectangle
            center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
            center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
            
            # Calculate Euclidean distance squared to (x,y)
            distance_squared = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
            
            # Check if this is better
            if distance_squared < best_distance_squared:
                best_distance_squared = distance_squared
                best_x1 = x1
                best_y1 = y1
            elif distance_squared == best_distance_squared:
                # Tie in distance - check lexicographic order
                if (x1, y1) < (best_x1, best_y1):
                    best_x1 = x1
                    best_y1 = y1
    
    print(best_x1, best_y1, best_x1 + width, best_y1 + height)

solve()
```

Wait, I see an issue with the time complexity. The ranges could be very large (up to 10^9). I need to optimize this.

## Optimized Solution

Since we want to minimize distance to (x,y), and the center is at ((x1+x2)/2, (y1+y2)/2) = ((x1 + x1 + width)/2, (y1 + y1 + height)/2), we can make the x-coordinate of center be x1 + width/2.

The distance squared becomes: (x1 + width/2 - x)² + (y1 + height/2 - y)²

To minimize this, we want:
- x1 as close as possible to x - width/2
- y1 as close as possible to y - height/2

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find maximum possible k such that k*a <= n and k*b <= m
max_k = min(n // a, m // b)

# Try to find largest k with valid positions
k = max_k
width = k * a
height = k * b

# Find valid ranges for x1 and y1
x1_min = max(0, x - width)
x1_max = min(x, n - width)
y1_min = max(0, y - height)
y1_max = min(y, m - height)

# If no valid k, try smaller values
while k > 0 and (x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max):
    k -= 1
    if k == 0:
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        exit()
    width = k * a
    height = k * b
    x1_min = max(0, x - width)
    x1_max = min(x, n - width)
    y1_min = max(0, y - height)
    y1_max = min(y, m - height)

# Now find optimal x1 and y1
best_x1 = x1_min
best_y1 = y1_min
best_distance_squared = float('inf')

# Optimal x1 should be closest to x - width/2
optimal_x1 = x1_min
if x1_min <= x1_max:
    # Find the x1 that minimizes |(x1 + width/2) - x|
    target_x = x - width / 2.0
    # The closest integer x1 in range [x1_min, x1_max]
    if target_x >= x1_min:
        optimal_x1 = int(max(x1_min, min(target_x, x1_max)))
    else:
        optimal_x1 = x1_min

# Optimal y1 should be closest to y - height/2
optimal_y1 = y1_min
if y1_min <= y1_max:
    target_y = y - height / 2.0
    if target_y >= y1_min:
        optimal_y1 = int(max(y1_min, min(target_y, y1_max)))
    else:
        optimal_y1 = y1_min

# Check if we can do better with other values (in case of tie)
best_x1 = optimal_x1
best_y1 = optimal_y1

# Ensure we're within bounds
x1 = best_x1
y1 = best_y1
x2 = x1 + width
y2 = y1 + height

# Verify constraints
if x1 < 0 or y1 < 0 or x2 > n or y2 > m:
    # Shouldn't happen with our bounds
    pass

print(x1, y1, x2, y2)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×m grid with coordinates from (0,0) to (n,m)
2. We need to find a sub-rectangle that:
   - Contains point (x,y)
   - Has aspect ratio a/b (width/height = a/b)
   - Maximizes the area (which means maximizing both width and height)
   - If multiple solutions, choose the one closest to (x,y) by Euclidean distance to center
   - If still multiple, choose lexicographically smallest

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Mathematical Constraints

For a sub-rectangle with width w = x₂ - x₁ and height h = y₂ - y₁, we need:
- w/h = a/b, so w = (a/b) × h
- w and h must be integers
- This means h must be divisible by b (so that w = (a×h)/b is integer)
- So h = k×b and w = k×a for some positive integer k

### Step 2: Constraints for Valid Rectangle

For rectangle (x₁,y₁,x₂,y₂):
- 0 ≤ x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ ≤ n → width w = x₂ - x₁ ≤ n - x₁ and w ≤ n - x, so w ≤ n - x₁
- 0 ≤ y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ ≤ m → height h = y₂ - y₁ ≤ m - y₁ and h ≤ m - y, so h ≤ m - y₁
- x₁ ≤ x ≤ x₂ → x₂ ≥ x, x₁ ≤ x
- y₁ ≤ y ≤ y₂ → y₂ ≥ y, y₁ ≤ y

### Step 3: Finding Maximum k

We need to find maximum k such that:
- width = k×a ≤ n - x₁ and x₂ = x₁ + k×a ≤ n, so x₁ ≤ n - k×a
- height = k×b ≤ m - y₁ and y₂ = y₁ + k×b ≤ m, so y₁ ≤ m - k×b
- x₁ ≤ x and x₁ + k×a ≥ x (so x₁ ≥ x - k×a)
- y₁ ≤ y and y₁ + k×b ≥ y (so y₁ ≥ y - k×b)

So we need:
- x₁ ≥ max(0, x - k×a)
- x₁ ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
- x₁ must satisfy max(0, x - k×a) ≤ min(x, n - k×a)

Similarly for y₁:
- y₁ ≥ max(0, y - k×b)
- y₁ ≤ min(y, m - k×b)
- y₁ must satisfy max(0, y - k×b) ≤ min(y, m - k×b)

For valid k, we need:
1. n - k×a ≥ 0 → k ≤ n/a
2. m - k×b ≥ 0 → k ≤ m/b
3. max(0, x - k×a) ≤ min(x, n - k×a) 
4. max(0, y - k×b) ≤ min(y, m - k×b)

### Step 4: Algorithm

1. Find maximum valid k = min(⌊n/a⌋, ⌊m/b⌋)
2. For this k, find valid x₁ and y₁ ranges
3. For x₁: must satisfy max(0, x - k×a) ≤ x₁ ≤ min(x, n - k×a)
4. For y₁: must satisfy max(0, y - k×b) ≤ y₁ ≤ min(y, m - k×b)
5. Choose x₁ to minimize distance to center, then lexicographically smallest

### Step 5: Distance to center

Center of rectangle is ((x₁+x₂)/2, (y₁+y₂)/2) = ((x₁+x₁+k×a)/2, (y₁+y₁+k×b)/2) = ((2x₁+k×a)/2, (2y₁+k×b)/2)

Distance² = ((2x₁+k×a)/2 - x)² + ((2y₁+k×b)/2 - y)²

We want to minimize this.

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Find maximum possible k
    max_k = min(n // a, m // b)
    
    if max_k == 0:
        # No valid rectangle possible
        print(0, 0, 0, 0)
        return
    
    # For maximum k, find valid ranges for x1 and y1
    # x1 must satisfy: max(0, x - k*a) <= x1 <= min(x, n - k*a)
    # y1 must satisfy: max(0, y - k*b) <= y1 <= min(y, m - k*b)
    
    k = max_k
    
    # Determine valid ranges
    x1_min = max(0, x - k * a)
    x1_max = min(x, n - k * a)
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - k * b)
    y1_max = min(y, m - k * b)
    
    # If no valid range, try smaller k
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        # Find maximum k where constraints are satisfied
        k = 0
        for k in range(max_k, 0, -1):
            x1_min = max(0, x - k * a)
            x1_max = min(x, n - k * a)
            y1_min = max(0, y - k * b)
            y1_max = min(y, m - k * b)
            
            if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
                break
        else:
            print(0, 0, 0, 0)
            return
    
    # Now we have valid k, find optimal x1, y1
    # We want to minimize distance to center
    # Distance squared: ((2*x1 + k*a)/2 - x)^2 + ((2*y1 + k*b)/2 - y)^2
    # = ((2*x1 + k*a - 2*x)/2)^2 + ((2*y1 + k*b - 2*y)/2)^2
    # = (x1 - x + k*a/2)^2 + (y1 - y + k*b/2)^2
    
    best_x1, best_y1 = x1_min, y1_min
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    
    # Check all valid (x1, y1) pairs
    # Since we want lexicographically smallest when distance is equal,
    # we'll check in order of x1, then y1
    
    # But we need to be efficient - the ranges might be large
    # However, the optimal x1 is likely close to x, and optimal y1 close to y
    
    # Try candidate values around optimal points
    candidates = []
    
    # Add endpoints
    for x1 in [x1_min, x1_max]:
        if x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max:
            for y1 in [y1_min, y1_max]:
                if y1_min <= y1 <= y1_max:
                    candidates.append((x1, y1))
    
    # Add some points around optimal center
    x_opt = x - k * a / 2
    y_opt = y - k * b / 2
    
    # Add points near the optimal x1 and y1
    for dx in [-1, 0, 1]:
        x_candidate = x1_min + dx
        if x1_min <= x_candidate <= x1_max:
            for dy in [-1, 0, 1]:
                y_candidate = y1_min + dy
                if y1_min <= y_candidate <= y1_max:
                    candidates.append((x_candidate, y_candidate))
    
    # Also add all points in the ranges if they're small
    if x1_max - x1_min <= 50 and y1_max - y1_min <= 50:
        for x1 in range(x1_min, x1_max + 1):
            for y1 in range(y1_min, y1_max + 1):
                candidates.append((x1, y1))
    
    # Sort by distance squared, then lexicographically
    candidates = sorted(candidates, key=lambda p: (p[0] - x)**2 + (p[1] - y)**2)
    
    # If multiple have same distance, choose lexicographically smallest
    candidates.sort(key=lambda p: (p[0], p[1]))
    
    # Find the one with minimum distance to center
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    best_x1, best_y1 = candidates[0]
    
    for x1, y1 in candidates:
        dist_sq = (x1 - (x - k * a / 2))**2 + (y1 - (y - k * b / 2))**2
        if dist_sq < best_dist_sq:
            best_dist_sq = dist_sq
            best_x1, best_y1 = x1, y1
        elif dist_sq == best_dist_sq:
            # Choose lexicographically smallest
            if (x1, y1) < (best_x1, best_y1):
                best_x1, best_y1 = x1, y1
    
    # But we need to be more systematic - the optimal x1 and y1 should be as close as possible to center
    # Center x should be around x + k*a/2, but constrained by x1_min and x1_max
    
    # Actually, let's be more careful with the optimization
    # We want to minimize (x1 - (x - k*a/2))^2 + (y1 - (y - k*b/2))^2
    # Since x1 and y1 are discrete, the optimal x1 is close to max(x1_min, x - k*a/2) and min(x1_max, x - k*a/2)
    
    # Find optimal x1 in range
    x_center = x - k * a / 2
    opt_x1 = int(round(x_center))
    if opt_x1 < x1_min:
        opt_x1 = x1_min
    elif opt_x1 > x1_max:
        opt_x1 = x1_max
    
    # Find optimal y1 in range
    y_center = y - k * b / 2
    opt_y1 = int(round(y_center))
    if opt_y1 < y1_min:
        opt_y1 = y1_min
    elif opt_y1 > y1_max:
        opt_y1 = y1_max
    
    # Check all candidates
    best_answer = None
    
    # Check boundary points and optimal points
    for x1 in [x1_min, x1_max, opt_x1]:
        if x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max:
            for y1 in [y1_min, y1_max, opt_y1]:
                if y1_min <= y1 <= y1_max:
                    x2 = x1 + k * a
                    y2 = y1 + k * b
                    center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2
                    center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2
                    dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                    
                    if best_answer is None or dist_sq < best_answer[0] or (dist_sq == best_answer[0] and (x1, y1, x2, y2) < best_answer[1]):
                        best_answer = (dist_sq, (x1, y1, x2, y2))
    
    # If we didn't find anything, try simple approach
    if best_answer is None:
        best_answer = (float('inf'), (x1_min, y1_min, x1_min + k*a, y1_min + k*b))
    
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = best_answer[1]
    
    print(x1, y1, x2, y2)
```

Wait, I think my approach is overcomplicating this. Let me reconsider.

Actually, since we want the maximum rectangle with aspect ratio a:b, we should first find the maximum possible k, then for that k, we need to find the best x1 and y1 within constraints.

```python
n, m, x, y, a, b = map(int, input().split())

# Find maximum possible k
k = min(n // a, m // b)

# If no valid k, output (0,0,0,0) - but this should be handled by constraints
if k == 0:
    print(0, 0, 0, 0)
else:
    # Get valid ranges for x1 and y1
    x1_min = max(0, x - k * a)
    x1_max = min(x, n - k * a)
    
    y1_min = max(0, y - k * b)
    y1_max = min(y, m - k * b)
    
    # If no valid ranges, try smaller k
    if x1_min > x1_max or y1_min > y1_max:
        # Find the maximum k where constraints hold
        k = 0
        for test_k in range(min(n // a, m // b), 0, -1):
            x1_min = max(0, x - test_k * a)
            x1_max = min(x, n - test_k * a)
            y1_min = max(0, y - test_k * b)
            y1_max = min(y, m - test_k * b)
            
            if x1_min <= x1_max and y1_min <= y1_max:
                k = test_k
                break
        
        if k == 0:
            print(0, 0, 0, 0)
            exit()
    
    # Now we have valid k, find best (x1, y1)
    # Center of rectangle is ((x1 + x1 + k*a)/2, (y1 + y1 + k*b)/2) = (x1 + k*a/2, y1 + k*b/2)
    # Distance to (x,y) is |x1 + k*a/2 - x| + |y1 + k*b/2 - y|
    # We want to minimize this
    
    # The optimal x1 should be as close as possible to x - k*a/2
    optimal_x1 = x - k * a / 2
    optimal_x1 = max(x1_min, min(x1_max, int(round(optimal_x1))))
    
    optimal_y1 = y - k * b / 2
    optimal_y1 = max(y1_min, min(y1_max, int(round(optimal_y1))))
    
    # But we need to check if this point is valid
    x1_best = optimal_x1
    y1_best = optimal_y1
    
    # Check all valid points in a small neighborhood
    candidates = []
    
    # Add the optimal point
    if x1_min <= optimal_x1 <= x1_max:
        candidates.append(optimal_x1)
    if x1_min <= x1_max:
        candidates.append(x1_min)
        candidates.append(x1_max)
    
    if y1_min <= optimal_y1 <= y1_max:
        candidates.append(optimal_y1)
    if y1_min <= y1_max:
        candidates.append(y1_min)
        candidates.append(y1_max)
    
    # Add points around the optimal values
    for dx in [-1, 0, 1]:
        x_cand = optimal_x1 + dx
        if x1_min <= x_cand <= x1_max:
            candidates.append(x_cand)
            
    for dy in [-1, 0, 1]:
        y_cand = optimal_y1 + dy
        if y1_min <= y_cand <= y1_max:
            candidates.append(y_cand)
    
    # Remove duplicates
    candidates = list(set(candidates))
    
    # Sort by distance to center, then lexicographically
    best_dist_sq = float('inf')
    best_x1, best_y1 = x1_min, y1_min
    
    for x1 in candidates:
        if x1_min <= x1 <= x1_max:
            for y1 in candidates:
                if y1_min <= y1 <= y1_max:
                    # Calculate center
                    center_x = x1 + k * a / 2.0
                    center_y = y1 + k * b / 2.0
                    dist_sq = (center_x - x) ** 2 + (center_y - y) ** 2
                    
                    if dist_sq < best_dist_sq:
                        best_dist_sq = dist_sq
                        best_x1, best_y1 = x1, y1
                    elif dist_sq == best_dist_sq:
                        # Choose lexicographically smaller
                        if (x1, y1) < (best_x1, best_y1):
                            best_x1, best_y1 = x1, y1
    
    # Calculate x2, y2
    x2 = best_x1 + k * a
    y2 = best_y1 + k * b
    
    print(int(best_x1), int(best_y1), int(x2), int(y2))
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Ma5termind and Subway are bored of their normal life.They want to do something interesting so that they can enjoy their last sem of college life.As usual Ma5termind comes up with a simple and interesting game.

Ma5termind gives Subway a compressed string. A compressed String is composed of characters and numbers.Every character in a compressed string contains a number. Ex a3b1c2 it represents aaabcc. Other compressed string could be a22b2a1 , z5c2x36. Each number in the compressed string indicates the number of times the corresponding character occurs in the string. The final string is 1 indexed.  Ma5termind wants Subway to sort the compressed string and tell him the kth character in the sorted compressed string.Since subway got speed Ma5termind asks him various such questions.

Help Subway in answering Ma5termind's question.

Input:

The first line contains the compressed string.This is followed by Q that indicates the number of questions Ma5termind shoots on Subway.Follows Q lines each line contain an integer K.

Output:

For every question output the Kth  character if it exists else print -1 .

Constraints:

2 ≤ Length of Compressed String ≤ 10^4

1 ≤ Length of Final String ≤ 10^18

1 ≤ Q ≤ 10^5

1 ≤ K ≤ 10^18

Every character in the string is lowercase English letter.

Note:

The number for a character may contain leading zeroes.

SAMPLE INPUT
a2b3c2a1

SAMPLE OUTPUT
a

b

a

c

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the characters in the final string
3. For each query K, we need to find the K-th character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- We need to extract characters and their frequencies
- The pattern is: character + number (which can have leading zeros)
- We'll iterate through the string and parse character-frequency pairs

### Step 2: Sort the characters
- Since we want the sorted string, we need to sort by character (alphabetically)

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- The final string length can be up to 10^18, so we can't generate the entire string
- We need to answer queries by finding which character corresponds to position K
- For each character, we calculate how many positions it occupies in the sorted string

### Step 4: Implementation approach
1. Parse the compressed string to get (character, frequency) pairs
2. Sort pairs by character
3. For each query K, iterate through sorted characters and find which character contains the K-th position

## Code Implementation

```python
def parse_compressed_string(compressed):
    result = []
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Parse the number
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        result.append((char, count))
    
    return result

def solve():
    # Read input
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string
    char_count_pairs = parse_compressed_string(compressed)
    
    # Sort by character (alphabetically)
    char_count_pairs.sort()
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is valid
        total_length = sum(count for _, count in char_count_pairs)
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character contains the k-th position
            current_pos = 0
            for char, count in char_count_pairs:
                if current_pos + count >= k:
                    # K falls within this character's range
                    print(char)
                    break
                current_pos += count
            else:
                # This should not happen given our validation
                print(-1)

solve()
```

## Explanation of the Algorithm

1. **Parsing**: Iterate through the compressed string, extracting each character and its frequency by reading digits until we hit a non-digit character.

2. **Sorting**: Sort the character-frequency pairs alphabetically by character.

3. **Query Processing**: For each query K:
   - First check if K exceeds the total length of the final string
   - If valid, iterate through sorted characters and find the range where K falls
   - The character that contains the K-th position is our answer

## Time Complexity
- Parsing: O(n) where n is length of compressed string
- Sorting: O(m log m) where m is number of unique characters
- Query processing: O(q × m) where m is number of unique characters

## Space Complexity
- O(m) for storing character-frequency pairs

The solution efficiently handles large K values (up to 10^18) without generating the entire string, making it suitable for the given constraints.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which represents "aaabbbcc" (a appears 2+1=3 times, b appears 3 times, c appears 2 times)
2. We need to sort this compressed string by characters (alphabetically)
3. For each query K, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted final string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- We need to extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers that can have multiple digits (like "22", "36")

### Step 2: Calculate total length
- Sum all frequencies to get the total length of the final string

### Step 3: Process queries
- For each query K, we need to find which character is at position K
- Since the string is sorted by characters, we can iterate through characters in alphabetical order and accumulate frequencies until we reach position K

### Step 4: Handle edge cases
- If K exceeds total length, return -1

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number that follows the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Update frequency count
        if char in freq:
            freq[char] += count
        else:
            freq[char] = count
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(freq.keys())
    
    # Calculate total length of final string
    total_length = sum(freq.values())
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # If K exceeds total length, return -1
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
        
        # Find which character is at position k
        current_pos = 0
        for char in sorted_chars:
            if current_pos + freq[char] >= k:
                # K falls within this character's range
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += freq[char]
        else:
            # This shouldn't happen if k <= total_length
            print(-1)

solve()
```

## Algorithm Explanation

1. **Parsing**: We iterate through the compressed string character by character. When we encounter a digit, we continue reading until we hit a non-digit, forming the complete number.

2. **Frequency counting**: We store the frequency of each character in a dictionary.

3. **Sorting**: We sort the characters alphabetically to get the final sorted string.

4. **Query processing**: For each query K:
   - Check if K exceeds total length (return -1 if yes)
   - Iterate through sorted characters, accumulating frequencies until we find which character contains position K
   - Return that character

## Time Complexity
- Parsing: O(n) where n is length of compressed string
- Query processing: O(26) per query (since we have at most 26 lowercase letters), so O(Q × 26) = O(Q)

## Space Complexity
- O(1) for frequency dictionary (at most 26 entries)

The solution handles leading zeros in numbers correctly and works efficiently even for large K values up to 10^18.

    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number that follows the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        
        # Update frequency count
        if char in freq:
            freq[char] += count
        else:
            freq[char] = count
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(freq.keys())
    
    # Calculate total length of final string
    total_length = sum(freq.values())
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # If K exceeds total length, return -1
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
        
        # Find which character is at position k
        current_pos = 0
        for char in sorted_chars:
            if current_pos + freq[char] >= k:
                # K falls within this character's range
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += freq[char]
        else:
            # This shouldn't happen if k <= total_length
            print(-1)

solve()

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which represents "aaabbbcc" (a appears 2+1=3 times, b appears 3 times, c appears 2 times)
2. We need to sort the final string lexicographically (alphabetically)
3. For each query K, we need to find the K-th character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds (greater than the total length), return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Go through each character and extract the number that follows
- Count how many times each character appears
- The number can have multiple digits (like "22" or "36")

### Step 2: Create frequency map
- Store each character and its frequency
- This gives us the distribution of characters in the final string

### Step 3: Sort characters
- Sort the characters alphabetically (a, b, c, ...)

### Step 4: Answer queries efficiently
- For each query K, we need to find which character appears at position K
- We can use prefix sums to efficiently find which character the K-th position belongs to

### Step 5: Handle edge cases
- If K exceeds the total length of the final string, return -1

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_count = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract all digits that follow the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        # Convert number string to integer
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        if count > 0:
            char_count[char] = char_count.get(char, 0) + count
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_count.keys())
    
    # Calculate prefix sums for efficient querying
    prefix_sum = []
    total_length = 0
    
    for char in sorted_chars:
        count = char_count[char]
        total_length += count
        
        if prefix_sum:
            prefix_sum.append(prefix_sum[-1] + count)
        else:
            prefix_sum = [count]
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is within bounds
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find which character contains the K-th position
        char_idx = 0
        for i, char in enumerate(sorted_chars):
            count = char_count[char]
            if k <= count:
                print(char)
                break
            k -= count
        else:
            print(-1)

solve()
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which represents "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to decode the compressed string and sort it alphabetically
3. For each query K, we need to find the K-th character in the sorted final string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Go through each character in the compressed string
- When we encounter a letter, extract all consecutive digits following it to get the count
- Store (character, count) pairs

### Step 2: Sort the characters
- Sort the pairs by character (alphabetically)

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- Since the final string length can be up to 10^18, we can't generate the entire string
- Instead, we need to find the K-th character by iterating through sorted character groups
- For each character group, check if K falls within its range

### Step 4: Implementation details
- Use a list to store (char, count) tuples
- Sort the list by character
- For each query K, iterate through the sorted groups and determine which group contains the K-th character

## Code Implementation

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string into (char, count) pairs
    pairs = []
    i = 0
    n = len(compressed)
    
    while i < n:
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract all digits following the character
        count = 0
        while i < n and compressed[i].isdigit():
            count = count * 10 + int(compressed[i])
            i += 1
        
        pairs.append((char, count))
    
    # Sort pairs by character (alphabetically)
    pairs.sort()
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Find the k-th character
        current_pos = 0
        
        for char, count in pairs:
            # Check if k falls within this character group
            if current_pos + count >= k:
                # K-th character is within this group
                # We need to find the (k - current_pos)-th character of this group
                result_pos = k - current_pos
                print(char)
                break
            else:
                # K is beyond this group, move to next group
                current_pos += count
        else:
            # K is beyond all characters
            print(-1)

solve()
```

## Explanation of the Algorithm

1. **Parsing**: We iterate through the compressed string character by character. When we find a letter, we extract all following digits to get its frequency.

2. **Sorting**: We sort the character-frequency pairs lexicographically (alphabetically).

3. **Query Processing**: For each query K:
   - We maintain a running position `current_pos`
   - For each character group, we check if K falls within the range `[current_pos, current_pos + count)`
   - If yes, we return the current character
   - If no, we add the count to current_pos and continue
   - If we exhaust all groups and K is still not found, we return -1

## Time Complexity
- Parsing: O(n) where n is length of compressed string
- Sorting: O(m log m) where m is number of unique characters
- Each query: O(m) where m is number of unique characters
- Total: O(n + m log m + Q * m)

## Space Complexity
- O(m) for storing character-frequency pairs

The solution handles the constraints well, especially the large string length and large K values by not generating the entire string.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which means "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the final string and answer Q queries about the Kth character
3. The final string is sorted alphabetically (lexicographically)
4. We need to handle large values of K (up to 10^18)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers with leading zeros (like "22" or "36")

### Step 2: Build frequency map
- Group characters by their frequency
- Store each character with its count

### Step 3: Sort the characters
- Sort characters alphabetically since we want lexicographical order

### Step 4: Answer queries efficiently
- Since K can be up to 10^18, we can't generate the entire string
- We need to find which character at position K by using prefix sums of frequencies

### Step 5: Implementation approach
1. Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
2. Sort characters alphabetically
3. For each query K, find which character is at position K using binary search or prefix sums

## Code Implementation

```python
import sys

def parse_compressed_string(s):
    """Parse compressed string and return character frequencies"""
    frequencies = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(s)
    
    while i < n:
        char = s[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract the number following the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and s[i].isdigit():
            num_str += s[i]
            i += 1
        
        if num_str:
            count = int(num_str)
            frequencies[char] = frequencies.get(char, 0) + count
    
    return frequencies

def solve():
    # Read input
    compressed_string = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get frequencies
    freq_map = parse_compressed_string(compressed_string)
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(freq_map.keys())
    
    # Create prefix sum array for efficiency
    prefix_sums = []
    cumulative_sum = 0
    
    for char in sorted_chars:
        count = freq_map[char]
        cumulative_sum += count
        prefix_sums.append((cumulative_sum, char))
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Find which character is at position k
        # Since we want 1-indexed position
        if k <= 0:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Binary search to find the range where k falls
        left, right = 0, len(prefix_sums) - 1
        result_char = -1
        
        while left <= right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            cumulative_sum_at_mid = prefix_sums[mid][0]
            
            if k <= cumulative_sum_at_mid:
                result_char = prefix_sums[mid][1]
                break
            else:
                left = mid + 1
        
        if result_char == -1:
            print(-1)
        else:
            print(result_char)

solve()
```

## How it works with sample input:

Input: "a2b3c2a1", queries: 2, 5, 1, 8

1. Parse frequencies: a→3, b→3, c→2
2. Sorted characters: ['a', 'b', 'c']
3. Prefix sums: 
   - a: 3 (cumulative: 3)
   - b: 6 (cumulative: 6)
   - c: 8 (cumulative: 8)

4. Query K=2: 2 ≤ 3 → answer is 'a'
5. Query K=5: 5 ≤ 6 → answer is 'b' 
6. Query K=1: 1 ≤ 3 → answer is 'a'
7. Query K=8: 8 ≤ 8 → answer is 'c'

This gives output: a, b, a, c ✓

The solution handles large values of K efficiently using binary search with O(log n) per query instead of O(n) for each query.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort this final string lexicographically (alphabetically)
3. For Q queries, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers that may have multiple digits (like "22")

### Step 2: Generate the sorted final string
- Sort the characters alphabetically
- For each character, we have its frequency

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- Since the final string can be up to 10^18 characters, we can't generate the entire string
- Instead, we need to find the Kth character by calculating cumulative frequencies

### Step 4: Algorithm design
1. Parse the input string to get character frequencies
2. Sort the characters alphabetically
3. For each query K:
   - Find the character whose cumulative frequency contains K
   - Return that character
   - If K exceeds total length, return -1

Let me trace through the example:
- "a2b3c2a1" → frequencies: a:3, b:3, c:2
- Sorted: a(3), b(3), c(2)
- Total length = 8
- K=2: First 3 positions are 'a', so 2nd character is 'a'
- K=5: Positions 4-6 are 'b', so 5th character is 'b' 
- K=1: First character is 'a'
- K=8: Last character is 'c'

## Implementation

```python
def parse_compressed(s):
    """Parse compressed string and return character frequencies"""
    freq = {}
    i = 0
    while i < len(s):
        char = s[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract number part
        num_str = ""
        while i < len(s) and s[i].isdigit():
            num_str += s[i]
            i += 1
        
        if num_str:
            count = int(num_str)
            freq[char] = freq.get(char, 0) + count
    
    return freq

def solve():
    # Read input
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get frequencies
    freq = parse_compressed(compressed)
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(freq.keys())
    
    # Calculate total length
    total_length = sum(freq.values())
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if K is valid
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character corresponds to Kth position
            cumulative = 0
            for char in sorted_chars:
                count = freq[char]
                if cumulative + count >= k:
                    print(char)
                    break
                cumulative += count
            else:
                # This should not happen if k <= total_length
                print(-1)

solve()
```

The solution works as follows:

1. **Parse function**: Extracts each character and its frequency by iterating through the string and collecting digit sequences.

2. **Frequency mapping**: Stores the count of each character.

3. **Query handling**: 
   - For each query K, we calculate cumulative frequencies in alphabetical order
   - We find the first character whose cumulative frequency range contains K
   - If K exceeds total length, we return -1

4. **Efficiency**: The solution is O(n + q * m) where n is compressed string length, q is number of queries, and m is number of unique characters. This is efficient even for large inputs.

The time complexity is acceptable because we don't generate the entire string (which can be up to 10^18 characters), but instead use cumulative frequency search to answer queries directly.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which means "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the final string (alphabetically) and answer Q queries about the kth character
3. The final string is sorted by characters (a, b, c, etc.)
4. We need to handle large values of K (up to 10^18) efficiently

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the compressed string
- Extract characters and their frequencies
- Handle numbers that may have leading zeros (like "22")

### Step 2: Sort characters alphabetically
- The final string will have characters in alphabetical order

### Step 3: Answer queries efficiently
- Since K can be up to 10^18, we can't generate the entire string
- We need to find which character is at position K by calculating cumulative frequencies

### Step 4: Algorithm
1. Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
2. Sort characters by their alphabetical order
3. For each query K:
   - Calculate cumulative frequency until we find which character contains K
   - Return that character or -1 if K exceeds total length

## Implementation

```python
def parse_compressed_string(s):
    """Parse compressed string and return character frequencies"""
    freq = {}
    i = 0
    n = len(s)
    
    while i < n:
        char = s[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract number part (can have leading zeros)
        num_str = ""
        while i < n and s[i].isdigit():
            num_str += s[i]
            i += 1
        
        if num_str:
            count = int(num_str)
            freq[char] = freq.get(char, 0) + count
    
    return freq

def solve():
    # Read input
    compressed_str = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse the compressed string to get frequencies
    char_freq = parse_compressed_string(compressed_str)
    
    # Sort characters alphabetically
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_freq.keys())
    
    # Calculate total length of final string
    total_length = sum(char_freq.values())
    
    # Process each query
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is valid
        if k > total_length:
            print(-1)
            continue
            
        # Find which character contains the kth position
        current_pos = 0
        for char in sorted_chars:
            count = char_freq[char]
            if current_pos + count >= k:
                # kth character is in this character's group
                print(char)
                break
            current_pos += count
        else:
            # This should not happen since k <= total_length
            print(-1)

solve()
```

## Explanation of the Algorithm

1. **Parsing**: We iterate through the string, extracting each character and its frequency by reading all consecutive digits.

2. **Frequency Calculation**: We build a dictionary of character frequencies.

3. **Sorting**: We sort the characters alphabetically since the final string is sorted.

4. **Query Processing**: For each query K:
   - We check if K exceeds the total length (if yes, return -1)
   - We iterate through characters in alphabetical order, accumulating their frequencies
   - When the cumulative frequency reaches or exceeds K, we know the Kth character is the current character

5. **Efficiency**: This solution is O(Q × m) where m is the number of unique characters, which is efficient even for large K values (up to 10^18).

The key insight is that we don't need to generate the entire string - we just need to determine which character contains the Kth position by using cumulative frequency sums.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a compressed string like "a2b3c2a1" which expands to "aaabbbcc"
2. We need to sort the final string lexicographically
3. For each query K, we need to find the Kth character in the sorted string
4. If K is out of bounds, return -1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the Compressed String
- Extract characters and their counts
- Handle numbers with leading zeros (like "22")
- Store pairs of (character, count)

### Step 2: Sort the Characters
- Sort characters lexicographically (a, b, c, ...)

### Step 3: Generate the Final String (Conceptually)
- We don't need to actually build the entire string (it could be up to 10^18 characters)
- Instead, we need to answer queries about the Kth character

### Step 4: Answer Queries Efficiently
- For each query K, we need to find which character is at position K
- We can use prefix sums of character counts to find the answer without generating the entire string

## Algorithm

1. Parse the compressed string to get character frequencies
2. Sort characters lexicographically
3. Calculate prefix sums of frequencies
4. For each query K:
   - Find which group (character) contains the Kth position
   - Return that character

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    compressed = input().strip()
    q = int(input().strip())
    
    # Parse compressed string to get character frequencies
    char_count = {}
    i = 0
    while i < len(compressed):
        char = compressed[i]
        i += 1
        
        # Extract number following the character
        num_str = ""
        while i < len(compressed) and compressed[i].isdigit():
            num_str += compressed[i]
            i += 1
        
        count = int(num_str) if num_str else 0
        if count > 0:
            char_count[char] = char_count.get(char, 0) + count
    
    # Sort characters lexicographically
    sorted_chars = sorted(char_count.keys())
    
    # Calculate prefix sums for efficient query answering
    prefix_sums = []
    current_sum = 0
    
    for char in sorted_chars:
        count = char_count[char]
        current_sum += count
        prefix_sums.append((char, current_sum))
    
    # Process queries
    for _ in range(q):
        k = int(input().strip())
        
        # Check if k is valid
        if k > prefix_sums[-1][1]:
            print(-1)
        else:
            # Find which character group contains position k
            for char, total_count in prefix_sums:
                if k <= total_count:
                    print(char)
                    break

solve()
```

## Trace through Sample Input

Compressed string: "a2b3c2a1"

1. Parse:
   - a: 2
   - b: 3  
   - c: 2
   - a: 1 (another a)
   - Final counts: a: 3, b: 3, c: 2

2. Sort characters: ['a', 'b', 'c']

3. Prefix sums:
   - a: 3 (total: 3)
   - b: 3 (total: 6)
   - c: 2 (total: 8)

4. Queries:
   - K=2: 2 ≤ 3, so answer is 'a'
   - K=5: 5 ≤ 6, so answer is 'b' 
   - K=1: 1 ≤ 3, so answer is 'a'
   - K=8: 8 ≤ 8, so answer is 'c'

This matches the expected output: a, b, a, c.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have binary strings of length N
2. For each string X, we run ZeroShark(X) which counts the number of adjacent "00" pairs
3. ZeroShark returns True only when there's exactly one "00" pair in the string
4. We need to count how many binary strings have exactly one "00" adjacent pair

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**For N=2:**
- "00": has one "00" pair → count = 1 → returns True
- "01": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → returns False  
- "10": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → returns False
- "11": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → returns False

Result: 1 string

**For N=3:**
- "000": has two "00" pairs (positions 0-1 and 1-2) → count = 2 → returns False
- "001": has one "00" pair (positions 0-1) → count = 1 → returns True
- "010": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → returns False
- "100": has one "00" pair (positions 1-2) → count = 1 → returns True
- "011": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → returns False
- "101": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → returns False
- "110": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → returns False
- "111": no "00" pairs → count = 0 → returns False

Result: 2 strings

## Pattern Recognition

For a string of length N, we need exactly one "00" pair.

The "00" pair can occur at positions (i, i+1) for i from 0 to N-2.

For a pair at position (i, i+1):
- We need x[i] = '0' and x[i+1] = '0'
- All other positions must not create additional "00" pairs

Let's think about this systematically:

1. **Case 1: The "00" pair is at position (i, i+1)**
   - x[i] = '0', x[i+1] = '0'
   - For positions < i: if any position j has x[j] = '0' and x[j+1] = '0', we get another "00" pair
   - Similarly for positions > i+1

2. **To avoid extra "00" pairs:**
   - For positions 0 to i-1: we need to ensure no "00" pairs exist
   - For positions i+2 to N-1: we need to ensure no "00" pairs exist
   - Position i+1 is already 0, so we need to ensure position i+2 (if exists) is not 0 when we check for "00" pairs

Wait, let me reconsider more carefully.

Actually, if we have a "00" pair at position (i, i+1), we need to ensure no other "00" pairs exist.

For positions < i: we need no "00" pairs
For positions > i+1: we need no "00" pairs

## Key Insight

For a "00" pair at position (i, i+1):
- Positions 0 to i-1: must not contain any "00" pairs
- Positions i+2 to N-1: must not contain any "00" pairs
- Position i+1 is fixed as '0', so we need to be careful about position i+2

Let me think about this differently. When we place a "00" pair at (i, i+1), we need to avoid any other "00" pairs.

For each possible position i where we can place the "00" pair:
- We have i positions before (0 to i-1): these must form a binary string with no "00" pairs
- We have (N-2-i) positions after (i+2 to N-1): these must form a binary string with no "00" pairs
- Position i+1 is '0', so position i+2 must be '1' to avoid creating another "00" pair at (i+1, i+2)

Actually, this gets complex. Let me think of the constraints again.

For a string to have exactly one "00" pair at position (i, i+1):
- x[i] = '0', x[i+1] = '0'
- For all j < i: no "00" pairs in positions (j, j+1)
- For all j > i+1: no "00" pairs in positions (j, j+1)
- Position i+2 must not be '0' (otherwise we'd have another "00" at (i+1, i+2))

Wait, if i+2 is '0', then (i+1, i+2) is "00" which would be another pair. So i+2 must be '1'.

## Final Pattern

For a "00" pair at position (i, i+1):
- The prefix (0 to i-1) must have no "00" pairs
- The suffix (i+2 to N-1) must have no "00" pairs  
- Position i+2 must be '1' (if i+2 exists)

The number of binary strings of length k with no "00" pairs is the Fibonacci sequence.

For length k, the number of strings with no "00" pairs follows the pattern:
- f(1) = 2 (0, 1)
- f(2) = 3 (01, 10, 11)
- f(3) = 5 (all except 000)

Actually, this is equivalent to the Fibonacci sequence.

## Pattern Derivation

For a string of length N, we need exactly one "00" pair.

For each possible position i (0 ≤ i ≤ N-2) where we place the "00" pair:
- The prefix of length i (positions 0 to i-1) must have no "00" pairs
- The suffix of length (N-2-i) (positions i+2 to N-1) must have no "00" pairs
- Position i+1 is '0'
- Position i+2 must be '1' (if i+2 < N)

When i+2 >= N, there's no position to check.

Let f(k) = number of binary strings of length k with no "00" pairs.
f(k) = f(k-1) + f(k-2) with f(1)=2, f(2)=3

Actually: 
- f(1) = 2 (0, 1)
- f(2) = 3 (01, 10, 11) - not 00

The constraint is that the prefix must have no "00" pairs, and we don't have constraints on individual characters beyond that.

For the prefix of length i, the number of valid strings is g(i).

For the suffix, if length m > 0, number of valid strings with first character '1' is g(m-1).

- i=0 (pair at 0,1): prefix length 0, suffix length 1 → need suffix to start with '1' → count = g(1-1) = g(0) = 1 → "001"

Let me think about the prefix.

For pair at i=1 (positions 1,2): 
- x[1]='0', x[2]='0'
- x[0] can be '0' or '1'
- But we need no "00" in prefix (0 to 0) - only one position, so no "00" pair
- For the suffix: length N-2-1 = 0, so no suffix

So strings: "000" and "100"

"000": has "00" at (0,1) and (1,2) → two "00" pairs → not allowed
"100": has "00" at (1,2) only → one "00" pair → allowed

So only "100" is valid.

The prefix constraint for length 1: x[0] can be '0' or '1' (2 choices), but we also need to avoid "00" pairs in prefix - for length 1, there are no adjacent pairs, so both '0' and '1' are allowed.

But in "000", we have "00" at (0,1) which is not allowed.

The issue is that when we have the pair at (1,2), and x[0]='0', then we have x[0]='0', x[1]='0' → "00" at (0,1) → extra pair.

So we need to ensure that when we place the pair at (i, i+1), there are no other "00" pairs.

If we have x[i]='0', and x[i-1]='0', then we have "00" at (i-1,i).

So for i=1, if x[0]='0', then x[0]='0', x[1]='0' → "00" at (0,1).

Therefore, to avoid "00" at (0,1), we need x[0]='1'.

So for pair at i=1: x[0]='1', x[1]='0', x[2]='0' → "100"

So only one string.

The pattern is that for a pair at (i,i+1), we need:
- x[i]='0', x[i+1]='0'
- For positions j < i: if x[j]='0', then x[j+1] must not be '0' to avoid "00" at (j,j+1)
- For positions j > i+1: if x[j]='0', then x[j-1] must not be '0' to avoid "00" at (j-1,j)

This is complex.

For a "00" pair at (i,i+1):
- We must have x[i]='0', x[i+1]='0'
- We must have x[i-1]='1' if i > 0 (to avoid "00" at (i-1,i))
- We must have x[i+2]='1' if i+2 < N (to avoid "00" at (i+1,i+2))
- The prefix (0 to i-1) must have no "00" pairs
- The suffix (i+2 to N-1) must have no "00" pairs

The prefix of length i (positions 0 to i-1) with no "00" pairs and the additional constraint that x[i-1]='1' if i > 0.

For the prefix of length i:
- If i = 0: no prefix, count = 1
- If i > 0: we need a string of length i with no "00" pairs and the last character is '1'

The number of such strings is g(i-1) where g(k) is the number of strings of length k with no "00" pairs.

Similarly for the suffix of length (N-2-i) (positions i+2 to N-1):
- If (N-2-i) = 0: count = 1
- If (N-2-i) > 0: we need a string of length (N-2-i) with no "00" pairs and the first character is '1' → count = g(N-3-i)

So total count = Σ(i=0 to N-2) [ (1 if i=0 else g(i-1)) * (1 if (N-2-i)=0 else g(N-3-i)) ]

For N=2:
- i=0: (1 if 0=0 else g(-1)) = 1, (1 if 0=0 else g(-1)) = 1 → 1
Result: 1 ✓

For N=3:
- i=0: (1) * (1 if 1=0 else g(-1)) = 1 * 1 = 1 (since N-2-0=1 > 0)
- i=1: (1 if 1>0 else g(0)) = g(0) = 1, (1 if 0=0 else g(-1)) = 1 → 1 * 1 = 1
- Total = 1 + 1 = 2 ✓

Perfect!

So the algorithm is:
1. Precompute g(k) for k from 0 to max_n, where g(k) = number of binary strings of length k with no "00" pairs
2. g(0) = 1
3. g(k) = g(k-1) + g(k-2) for k >= 2, with g(1) = 2
4. For each N, compute sum over i=0 to N-2 of: 
   - prefix_count = 1 if i == 0 else g(i-1)
   - suffix_count = 1 if (N-2-i) == 0 else g(N-3-i)
   - total += prefix_count * suffix_count

Let me verify the recurrence:
- g(0) = 1
- g(1) = 2 (0, 1)
- g(2) = 3 (01, 10, 11) - 00 is excluded
- g(3) = g(2) + g(1) = 3 + 2 = 5
- g(4) = g(3) + g(2) = 5 + 3 = 8

Yes, this is the Fibonacci sequence shifted to start with g(0)=1, g(1)=2.

g(k) = F_{k+2} where F_1=1, F_2=1, F_3=2, F_4=3, F_5=5...

Now I'll implement this.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    # Precompute g(k) for k from 0 to 100000
    max_n = 100000
    g = [0] * (max_n + 1)
    g[0] = 1
    if max_n >= 1:
        g[1] = 2
    for i in range(2, max_n + 1):
        g[i] = (g[i-1] + g[i-2]) % MOD
    
    T = int(input())
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        if N == 1:
            print(0)
        else:
            total = 0
            # i from 0 to N-2
            for i in range(0, N - 1):
                # prefix_count: for positions 0 to i-1
                if i == 0:
                    prefix_count = 1
                else:
                    prefix_count = g[i-1]  # last character of prefix must be '1' to avoid "00" at (i-1,i)
                
                # suffix_count: for positions i+2 to N-1
                suffix_len = N - 2 - i
                if suffix_len == 0:
                    suffix_count = 1
                else:
                    # suffix starts at i+2, must start with '1' to avoid "00" at (i+1,i+2)
                    suffix_count = g[suffix_len - 1]
                
                total = (total + prefix_count * suffix_count) % MOD
            
            print(total)

solve()
```

def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    # Precompute g(k) for k from 0 to 100000
    max_n = 100000
    g = [0] * (max_n + 1)
    g[0] = 1
    if max_n >= 1:
        g[1] = 2
    for i in range(2, max_n + 1):
        g[i] = (g[i-1] + g[i-2]) % MOD
    
    T = int(input())
    for _ in range(T):
        N = int(input())
        if N == 1:
            print(0)
        else:
            total = 0
            # i from 0 to N-2
            for i in range(0, N - 1):
                # prefix_count: for positions 0 to i-1
                if i == 0:
                    prefix_count = 1
                else:
                    prefix_count = g[i-1]  # last character of prefix must be '1' to avoid "00" at (i-1,i)
                
                # suffix_count: for positions i+2 to N-1
                suffix_len = N - 2 - i
                if suffix_len == 0:
                    suffix_count = 1
                else:
                    # suffix starts at i+2, must start with '1' to avoid "00" at (i+1,i+2)
                    suffix_count = g[suffix_len - 1]
                
                total = (total + prefix_count * suffix_count) % MOD
            
            print(total)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

Given T testcases, where each testcase consists of integer N, calculate the length of binTrues for each N. Because the lists can get large, output your answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

INPUT

The first line of the input contains a single integer T, the number of such N.

The next T lines of the input contain a single integer N, as described in the problem.

OUTPUT

Output T separate lines, each containing the number of elements in the resulting list binTrues for each N in the input. The lists may be large, so output the answer modulo 10^9+7=1000000007

CONSTRAINTS

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

1 ≤ N ≤ 10^5

SAMPLE INPUT
2

2

3

SAMPLE OUTPUT
1

2

Explanation

When N=2, the list binStrings is equal to ['00', '01', '10', '11'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 00. When X = 01, 10, 11, the counter in ZeroShark is equal to 0. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['00'], and has a length of 1.

When N=3, the list binStrings is equal to ['000', '001', '010', '100', '011', '101', '110', '111'].

Calling ZeroShark on each element in binStrings, the function only returns true when X is equal to 001 or 100. 

Therefore, binTrues is equal to ['001', '100'], and has a length of 2.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

But "1100" has "00" at position 2 (indices 2,3) → only one pair
"0100" has "00" at position 2 (indices 2,3) → only one pair

So valid strings for N=4 are:
- "0010": positions 0,1='00', position 2='1', position 3='0' → "0010" has "00" at position 1 only
- "0011": "00" at position 1 only
- "1001": "00" at position 2 only
- "0100": "00" at position 3 only
- "1100": "00" at position 3 only

That's 5 strings.

So the pattern is:
For each possible position i (1 to n-1) where we place a "00" pair:
- x[i-1] = '0', x[i] = '0'
- If i-2 >= 0: x[i-2] = '1' (to prevent "00" at i-2)
- If i+1 < n: x[i+1] = '1' (to prevent "00" at i+1)
- All other positions can be 0 or 1, but we need to ensure they don't create additional "00" pairs.

Actually, in the cases above, we don't need to worry about other zeros because we're constraining the immediate neighbors. The constraint ensures no adjacent "00" pairs.

But is that sufficient? What about positions that are not adjacent to our pair?

In the "0010" example, positions 0,1 are "00", position 2 is "1", position 3 is "0". The only "00" pair is at position 1.

Yes, because if we have x[j]='0' for any j not in the pair, and it's adjacent to another '0', we'd have a "00" pair. But our constraints ensure that any position adjacent to the pair is set to 1.

For positions not adjacent to the pair, we can freely assign 0 or 1, because if we have two adjacent 0s, they would have formed a "00" pair earlier, but our constraints should cover that.

Actually, let me think again. The only way to have a "00" pair is between adjacent positions. We're ensuring that:
- At position i-2 (if exists): not both 0 (by setting i-2 to 1)
- At position i+1 (if exists): not both 0 (by setting i+1 to 1)
- Any other "00" pair would occur elsewhere, which we're not constraining.

Oh wait, that's wrong. I'm not constraining other potential "00" pairs.

For example, if I have "0100" in N=4:
- "0" at position 0, "1" at 1, "0" at 2, "0" at 3
- "00" at position 2,3 only → good
- But what if I have "0001" with a "00" pair at position 1,2? That would have multiple "00" pairs.

I think I need to rethink.

Actually, let me go back to the original problem. The function ZeroShark counts ALL instances of "00" pairs. We need exactly one such instance.

So the string must have exactly one contiguous "00" substring of length 2.

For a string to have exactly one "00" pair:
- It must contain exactly one occurrence where two consecutive positions are both '0'
- This means the zeros are in exactly one adjacent pair

This happens when:
- There is a contiguous segment of exactly two zeros, and all other zeros are isolated (single zeros)
- Or there is one zero followed immediately by another zero, and no other adjacent zeros

More precisely:
- The string contains exactly one occurrence of "00" 
- All other adjacent pairs are not both '0'

This means the zeros form exactly one pair, and all other zeros are singletons with 1s around them.

For a string of length N:
- Position i can be where the "00" pair starts (0-indexed)
- For the pair at position i: positions i and i+1 are both '0'
- To ensure no other "00" pairs:
  - If i > 0: position i-1 must be '1' (to avoid "00" at i-1,i)
  - If i+2 < n: position i+2 must be '1' (to avoid "00" at i+1,i+2)
- All other positions can be anything, but we need to ensure no other "00" pairs are formed.

Actually, this is getting very complex. Let me check known patterns or derive a formula.

Looking at the examples:
N=2: answer = 1
N=3: answer = 2  
N=4: from my calculation above, I got 5

Let me verify if 5 is correct for N=4:
All 4-bit strings with exactly one "00" pair:

1. "0010": positions 0,1="00" → only one pair ✅
2. "0011": positions 0,1="00" → only one pair ✅  
3. "1001": positions 1,2="00" → only one pair ✅
4. "1100": positions 2,3="00" → only one pair ✅
5. "0100": positions 2,3="00" → only one pair ✅

Are there more?

So only 5 strings.

So the pattern is:
For each possible start position i of the "00" pair (i from 0 to n-2):
- Positions i and i+1 are '0'
- If i > 0: position i-1 is '1' (to avoid "00" at i-1,i)
- If i+2 < n: position i+2 is '1' (to avoid "00" at i+1,i+2)
- The remaining positions are free to be 0 or 1, BUT we need to ensure no other "00" pairs

Wait, are the remaining positions really free? In "0010", we have position 3='0', and it's not adjacent to another '0', so it's fine. In "0100", position 0='0' and position 1='1', so no issue.

The only way to have another "00" pair is if there are two adjacent zeros not at the main pair location. 

But with the constraints we've placed (i-1='1' when i>0, i+2='1' when i+2<n), we've prevented "00" at positions i-1,i and i+1,i+2.

For any other potential "00" pair (j,j+1) where j is not i-1,i,i+1,i+2:
- We need to ensure that at least one of x[j] or x[j+1] is '1'

But this is not guaranteed by our constraints. For example, if we have "0101" and we place a "00" pair at position 1,2, we might have other zeros.

Actually, I think I need to be more careful. The constraints only prevent "00" pairs at specific adjacent positions. For positions not adjacent to our pair, we might accidentally create "00" pairs.

However, in the examples, when we assigned freely, it still worked. Let me think of a case where it fails.

Suppose N=5, pair at position 1 (indices 1,2):

- If x[4]='1': string is "10011" → only one pair ✅

If x[4]='0', is there a "00" pair? Only at (1,2). The zero at position 4 is isolated.

The key point is that without adjacent zeros, there can't be a "00" pair.

So if we ensure that at positions i-1 and i+2 (when they exist) we have '1', and all positions are otherwise free, then:
- The only "00" pair is at i,i+1
- All other pairs are either "01" or "10" or "11"

Wait, is that true? What if we have "00110" with a "00" pair at position 0,1:
- We require x[1]='0' (good), x[2]='1' (good)
- x[0]='0', x[1]='0', x[2]='1', x[3]='1', x[4]='0'

Therefore, for each possible position i (0 to n-2) for the "00" pair:
- Fix positions i and i+1 to '0'
- If i > 0: fix position i-1 to '1'
- If i+2 < n: fix position i+2 to '1'
- All other positions can be 0 or 1 → 2^(n-4) possibilities if i>0 and i+2<n, 2^(n-3) if only one constraint, 2^(n-2) if no constraint

More precisely:
- Number of free positions = n - (number of fixed positions)
- Fixed positions: i, i+1, (i-1 if i>0), (i+2 if i+2<n)
- So positions fixed: 2 positions (i,i+1) + 0,1 or 0,0 or 0,1 or 1,0

Actually, the fixed positions are:
- Always: i and i+1
- If i > 0: i-1
- If i+2 < n: i+2

So number of fixed positions = 2 + (1 if i>0) + (1 if i+2 < n)

Therefore, number of free positions = n - (2 + (1 if i>0) + (1 if i+2 < n))

Answer for each N is sum over i from 0 to n-2 of 2^(n - 2 - (1 if i>0) - (1 if i+2 < n))

Let me calculate for small cases:

For n>=5: (n-3)*2^(n-4)

Final code:

```python
MOD = 1000000007

def solve(n):
    if n == 1:
        return 0
    elif n == 2:
        return 1
    else:
        term1 = pow(2, n-2, MOD)
        if n == 3:
            term2 = 0
        else:
            term2 = (n - 3) * pow(2, n-4, MOD)
        return (term1 + term2) % MOD

T = int(input())
for _ in range(T):
    N = int(input())
    print(solve(N))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A binary string of length N is a string of N characters, where each character is either 0 or 1. 

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

So the formula is:
Answer(N) = Σ(i=0 to N-2) F(i+1) × F(N-i-1)

Where F(k) is the k-th Fibonacci number with F(1)=1, F(2)=1, F(3)=2, etc.

We can optimize this by precomputing Fibonacci numbers and then for each N, summing the products.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    # Precompute Fibonacci numbers up to 10^5
    max_n = 100000
    fib = [0] * (max_n + 2)
    fib[1] = 1
    fib[2] = 1
    for i in range(3, max_n + 2):
        fib[i] = (fib[i-1] + fib[i-2]) % MOD
    
    t = int(input())
    for _ in range(t):
        n = int(input())
        if n < 2:
            print(0)
        else:
            result = 0
            # Sum over i from 0 to n-2
            for i in range(n - 1):
                term = (fib[i + 1] * fib[n - i - 1]) % MOD
                result = (result + term) % MOD
            print(result)

solve()
```

View Extracted Code 
def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    # Precompute Fibonacci numbers up to 10^5
    max_n = 100000
    fib = [0] * (max_n + 2)
    fib[1] = 1
    fib[2] = 1
    for i in range(3, max_n + 2):
        fib[i] = (fib[i-1] + fib[i-2]) % MOD
    
    t = int(input())
    for _ in range(t):
        n = int(input())
        if n < 2:
            print(0)
        else:
            result = 0
            # Sum over i from 0 to n-2
            for i in range(n - 1):
                term = (fib[i + 1] * fib[n - i - 1]) % MOD
                result = (result + term) % MOD
            print(result)

solve()

Wet Shark makes a list binStrings, consisting of all 2^N N-digit binary strings. For each string X in binStrings, Wet Shark runs a function ZeroShark(X), defined as follows (psuedocode):

bool ZeroShark(x):
//note the zero indexing
n = length(x)
counter = 0
FOR i = 1 to n-1
    IF x[i] == '0' and x[i - 1] == '0'
        INCREMENT counter
        END IF
ENDFOR
return (counter == 1)

Wet Shark stores an element X in a new list, binTrues  if ZeroShark(X) returns True for that X . In otherwords, binTrues consists of all the elements X in binStrings such that ZeroShark(X) returns True.

1 ≤ T ≤ 10

## Problem Understanding

1. We have binary strings of length N
2. For each string X, we run ZeroShark(X) which counts the number of adjacent "00" pairs (where both positions i and i-1 have value '0')
3. ZeroShark(X) returns True only when there's exactly one "00" pair
4. We need to count how many binary strings have exactly one "00" pair

## Analysis

Let me analyze what constitutes exactly one "00" pair in a binary string:

For a string of length N, positions are 0, 1, 2, ..., N-1.
We need exactly one occurrence where both position i and position i-1 are '0' (for i from 1 to N-1).

Let me think about valid patterns:

**Case 1: Exactly one "00" pair**
- This means there's exactly one index i (from 1 to N-1) such that x[i] = '0' and x[i-1] = '0'
- All other adjacent pairs must not be "00"

**Key observations:**
1. If we have a "00" pair starting at position j (meaning x[j] and x[j-1] are both '0'), then:
   - We can't have another "00" pair
   - We can't have x[j] = '0' and x[j+1] = '0' (would create another pair)

2. The "00" pair must be isolated - meaning:
   - Position j-1 must not be '0' if j > 1 (to avoid a pair starting at j-1)
   - Position j+1 must not be '0' if j < N-1 (to avoid a pair starting at j+1)

- If i = 1: we have positions 0,1 both '0'
- If i = 2: we have positions 1,2 both '0'
- ...
- If i = N-1: we have positions N-2, N-1 both '0'

For each possible position of the "00" pair, we need to ensure:
- No other "00" pairs exist

## Correct Analysis

For a "00" pair at position j (meaning x[j-1]='0' and x[j]='0', where j from 1 to N-1):
- The pair exists

  - If j < N-1, then x[j+1] must not be '0' (to prevent pair at j+1), so x[j+1] = '1'
- All other positions can be anything

- Total constraints: positions j-1, j, and possibly j-2, j+1
- The count is 2^(number of free positions)

Number of free positions:
- Total positions: N
- Positions that are fixed: 
  - j-1, j (always fixed)
  - j-2 (if j > 1)
  - j+1 (if j < N-1)

So:
- If j = 1: fixed positions: 0,1 (since j-2 = -1 invalid). If N > 2, we also fix x[2]='1' to prevent pair at position 2. So fixed positions: 0,1,2 → free positions: N-3

## Final Formula

For a given N:
- If N = 1: no possible "00" pairs (need at least 2 positions), so answer = 0
- If N >= 2:
  - For j = 1: count = 2^(N-3) if N > 2, else 1 (if N=2)
  - For j = N-1: count = 2^(N-3) if N > 2, else 1 (if N=2)
  - For j from 2 to N-2: count = 2^(N-4) if N > 4, else 0

More precisely:
- If N = 1: answer = 0
- If N = 2: only j=1, count = 1 (2^0)
- If N = 3: j=1 and j=2, each contributes 1 (2^0), total = 2
- If N >= 4:
  - j=1: contributes 2^(N-3)
  - j=N-1: contributes 2^(N-3) 
  - j=2 to N-2: there are (N-4) such j values, each contributes 2^(N-4)
  - Total = 2 * 2^(N-3) + (N-4) * 2^(N-4) = 2^(N-2) + (N-4) * 2^(N-4)
